⏺ All 37 assertions pass. The index scan approach works:

  Before (MARK join):
  TABLE_SCAN (10M rows) → HASH_JOIN (MARK) → FILTER → 9K rows
  Every row scanned. Row groups never skipped.

  After (index scan):
  LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN (9K rows) → FILTER → 9K rows
  Only matching rows fetched via DataTable::Fetch(). Row groups without matching
  row IDs are never touched. Timestamp pruning further reduces the row IDs
  before fetching.

✻ Cogitated for 11m 7s

❯ Summarize your changes                                                        

⏺ What changed

  New file: logsearch_index_scan.cpp/hpp

  Custom table function that fetches rows by row ID using DataTable::Fetch().

  - Bind data: stores table name + sorted row IDs from the inverted index
  - Global state: resolves DataTable reference, converts column indices to
  storage indices, tracks progress with atomic counter for parallel fetch
  - Scan function: grabs batches of STANDARD_VECTOR_SIZE row IDs, calls
  storage.Fetch(tx, output, column_ids, row_id_vector, count, fetch_state)
  - Row groups with no matching row IDs are never touched — Fetch() routes
  directly to the row group containing each row ID

  Changed: logsearch_optimizer.cpp

  Replaced the MARK join approach with LogicalGet replacement.

  Before: Created a MARK join between the table scan and an in-memory table of
  row IDs. Table scan still read all rows; join tagged matches.

  After: Replaces the LogicalGet node's function with logsearch_index_scan. Same
  table_index preserved so all column bindings remain valid. Original filter
  expressions kept on top for correctness.

  auto new_get = make_uniq<LogicalGet>(
      get.table_index,           // same — bindings stay valid
      index_scan_func,           // our function
      bind_data_with_row_ids,    // row IDs from index
      get.returned_types,        // same columns
      get.names, get.virtual_columns
  );
  new_get->SetColumnIds(get.GetColumnIds());  // same projections
  filter.children[0] = std::move(new_get);

  Also fixed in optimizer (from earlier debugging)

  1. pre_optimize_function instead of optimize_function — runs before filter
  pushdown removes LogicalFilter nodes
  2. Flatten AND conjunctions — contains(msg, 'x') AND ts > y is one
  conjunction, not two expressions
  3. Handle CAST-wrapped constants — ts > CAST('2024-01-04' AS TIMESTAMP)
  resolved via IsFoldable() + TryEvaluateScalar()
  4. Column name resolution — resolve through column_ids → names, not direct
  index into names

  Plan shape comparison

  OLD:  AGGREGATE → FILTER → PROJECTION → HASH_JOIN(MARK) → [TABLE_SCAN(all
  rows), COLUMN_DATA_SCAN(row IDs)]
  NEW:  AGGREGATE → FILTER → LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN(only matching rows)

  Row group skipping verified

  ┌──────────────────────────────┬────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │            Query             │   Rows     │       vs. full scan        │
  │                              │  fetched   │                            │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ contains(msg, 'error') on    │ 50,000     │ 10% (index selectivity)    │
  │ 500K rows                    │            │                            │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Same + ts > '2024-01-04'     │ 25,424     │ 5% (ts pruning skips       │
  │                              │            │ partitions)                │
  └──────────────────────────────┴────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

❯ Do the row IDs need to be in bind_data? Wouldn't it make more sense to fetch  
  them from the index during execution?                                         

⏺ You're right. Current approach materializes all row IDs at optimization time —
  wrong for two reasons:

  1. Memory: 1M matching rows = 8MB of row IDs allocated during planning, held
  until execution
  2. Staleness: Plan is cached/reused, but index content may change between
  planning and execution (new inserts)
  3. No laziness: Can't short-circuit for LIMIT — all row IDs computed upfront
  even if only 10 needed

  Better: store an index reference + search parameters in bind data. Fetch row
  IDs lazily during execution.

  // Before (current)
  struct LogsearchScanBindData {
      shared_ptr<vector<row_t>> row_ids;  // materialized at plan time
  };

  // After (better)  
  struct LogsearchScanBindData {
      string catalog_name, schema_name, table_name;

      // Search parameters — evaluated during execution, not planning
      LogsearchIndex *index;              // pointer to the index
      vector<string> search_terms;        // what to search for
      string search_prefix;               // for prefix search
      bool is_prefix;
      timestamp_t ts_min, ts_max;
      bool has_ts_bounds;
  };

  Then in the scan function's global init (execution time, not plan time):

  // Query the index HERE, not in the optimizer
  auto row_ids = index->SearchTerms(terms, ts_min, ts_max, has_ts_bounds);
  gstate->row_ids = std::move(row_ids);

  Benefits:
  - Row IDs computed at execution time — fresh data, correct transaction
  snapshot
  - Could make this lazy per-partition: only fetch row IDs for the next
  partition, enabling early termination for LIMIT
  - Selectivity check still happens at optimizer time (cheap — just check
  posting list sizes)
  - Memory only used during execution, freed when query finishes

  One concern: Storing a raw pointer to LogsearchIndex* in bind data. The index
  could be dropped between planning and execution. Need either:
  - A shared_ptr to the index (prevents drop during query)
  - Or look up the index by name at execution time (safer but slower)

  Want me to implement this?

❯ Why did you decide to use a table function instead of a physical operator?    
  How does the LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN compare to the ART index scan?              

⏺ Why table function instead of physical operator

  Pragmatism. A custom PhysicalOperator subclass would need:
  - Integration with the physical plan generator
  (PhysicalPlanGenerator::CreatePlan)
  - A corresponding LogicalOperator subclass
  - Serialization support
  - Pipeline integration (source/sink/operator classification)
  - Proper parallelism integration with DuckDB's task scheduler

  A table function gets all of this for free — DuckDB wraps it in
  PhysicalTableScan automatically. Registration is one line. The physical plan
  generator already knows how to handle LogicalGet with any table function.

  Tradeoff: Less control. A physical operator could integrate deeper — e.g.,
  push filters through, participate in join ordering, provide statistics to the
  optimizer. The table function is a black box to the optimizer.

  How LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN compares to ART index scan

  They're architecturally different:

  Aspect: Where it lives
  ART index scan: Inside table_scan.cpp — same physical operator as regular scan
  LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN: Separate table function — replaces the LogicalGet
    entirely
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Aspect: Decision point
  ART index scan: Runtime (in TableScanInitGlobal)
  LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN: Optimizer time (in pre_optimize_function)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Aspect: Row ID source
  ART index scan: ART tree traversal during execution
  LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN: Inverted index query (currently at plan time, should be
    at execution time)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Aspect: Local storage
  ART index scan: Scans local storage (uncommitted inserts) in a second phase
  LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN: Doesn't see local storage at all — only committed data
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Aspect: Row ID limit
  ART index scan: Capped at index_scan_max_count (2048) or 0.1% of table. Falls
    back to full scan if too many
  LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN: 20% selectivity threshold, but no hard cap on row count
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Aspect: Integration
  ART index scan: Uses same TableScanBindData — seamless, same operator
  LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN: Replaces the scan — downstream operators see a different

    function
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Aspect: Filter pushdown
  ART index scan: Remaining filters pushed into the same scan operator
  LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN: Filters stay in LogicalFilter above — can't be pushed
    into our function
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Aspect: Statistics
  ART index scan: Optimizer has full table statistics
  LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN: Optimizer only sees our cardinality estimate
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Aspect: Fetch path
  ART index scan: Same DataTable::Fetch()
  LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN: Same DataTable::Fetch()

  The key difference

  ART's approach is runtime, inside the existing scan operator. The optimizer
  doesn't know an index scan is happening. The PhysicalTableScan decides at init
  time: "I have an ART index that matches this filter, and it returns few
  enough rows — I'll use it." The scan operator handles both the index path and
  the regular path.

  Our approach is optimizer-time, replacing the scan operator. The optimizer
  sees the inverted index, decides to use it, and swaps the scan function. By
  execution time, the plan is already committed to the index path.

  What ART gets right that we don't

  1. Local storage scanning: ART index scan has a second phase that scans
  uncommitted local inserts. We miss these entirely.
  2. Graceful fallback: If ART returns too many rows, it falls back to full scan
  in the same operator. We're committed at optimizer time.
  3. Filter integration: The remaining (non-index) filters are in the same
  TableScanBindData and can use zone maps. Our remaining filters are in a
  LogicalFilter above — no zone map pruning for them.

  What we get right that ART doesn't

  1. Multi-token boolean queries: ART handles single-column point/range lookups.
  We handle contains(msg, 'error') which requires tokenization + posting list
  intersection.
  2. Timestamp pruning at the index level: We skip entire index partitions
  before even producing row IDs. ART doesn't do cross-column pruning.
  3. No row count cap: ART caps at 2048 rows / 0.1%. We allow up to 20%
  selectivity, which can be millions of rows. (Whether this is "right" is
  debatable.)

  The ideal long-term approach

  Integrate into table_scan.cpp like ART does — a DuckLogsearchIndexScanState
  that:
  1. Decides at runtime whether to use the index
  2. Queries the inverted index lazily during execution
  3. Falls back to full scan if too many matches
  4. Handles local storage
  5. Lets remaining filters use zone maps

  This requires generalizing table_scan.cpp beyond ART (currently hardcoded),
  which means modifying DuckDB core. The table function approach avoids that.

✻ Baked for 37s

❯ 3. Filter integration: The remaining (non-index) filters are in the same      
  TableScanBindData and can use zone maps. Our remaining filters are in a       
  LogicalFilter above — no zone map                                             
    pruning for them.                                                           
  This sounds like a big one. In log search, you want to push down filters for  
  high-cardinality attributes like user_id                                      

⏺ Right. Consider:

  SELECT * FROM logs
  WHERE contains(message, 'timeout')
    AND user_id = 'usr_abc123'
    AND ts > '2024-01-04'

  Current behavior:
  1. Optimizer: inverted index finds 25K rows with "timeout" + ts pruning
  2. LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN fetches all 25K rows (all columns)
  3. FILTER above checks user_id = 'usr_abc123' → 3 rows survive
  4. Zone maps on user_id never consulted — fetched 25K rows, needed 3

  With ART-style integration:
  1. Index scan produces 25K row IDs
  2. Same scan operator has user_id = 'usr_abc123' as a pushed-down filter
  3. Zone maps prune row groups where user_id min/max doesn't contain
  'usr_abc123'
  4. Fetch only rows from surviving row groups — maybe 1K instead of 25K

  The zone map prune is the big win. For high-cardinality columns like user_id,
  zone maps may not help much (the value could be in any row group). But for
  medium-cardinality columns like service, region, level — zone maps are very
  effective and we're losing that entirely.

  Three ways to fix this, increasing difficulty:

  Option 1: Post-fetch filtering inside our table function (easy)

  Accept pushed-down filters in our table function. Apply them after Fetch() but
  before returning rows. No zone map benefit, but avoids the overhead of an
  extra FILTER operator and its projections.

  func.filter_pushdown = true;
  // In scan function: after Fetch, apply filters to output chunk

  Marginal improvement. Doesn't solve the zone map problem.

  Option 2: Group row IDs by row group, apply zone maps ourselves (medium)

  Before fetching, group row IDs by row group. For each row group, check zone
  maps against the remaining filters. Skip row groups that don't match.

  // In global init:
  // 1. Get row IDs from index: [100, 200, 150000, 150100, ...]
  // 2. Group by row group: {rg0: [100, 200], rg1: [150000, 150100], ...}
  // 3. For each row group, check zone maps for pushed-down filters
  //    - rg0: user_id zone map [usr_aaa, usr_zzz] → contains 'usr_abc123'? 
  maybe → keep
  //    - rg1: user_id zone map [usr_maa, usr_mzz] → contains 'usr_abc123'? no →
  SKIP
  // 4. Only fetch from surviving row groups

  This gives us zone map pruning. Requires accessing row group statistics from
  within the table function. The DataTable has this via GetPartitionStats().

  Option 3: Integrate into table_scan.cpp (hard, correct)

  The proper solution. Requires DuckDB core changes to generalize beyond ART.

  ---
  Option 2 is the right balance — zone map pruning without core changes. Want me
  to implement it?

❯ > For high-cardinality columns like user_id, zone maps may not help much (the 
  value could be in any row group). But for medium-cardinality columns          
     like service, region, level — zone maps are very effective and we're       
  losing that entirely.                                                         
  Wait, why? user_id is high-cardinality, so it is not in many row groups. So   
  in the optimal case, many row groups can be skipped.                          

⏺ You're right, I was confused. Let me correct:

  High cardinality = many distinct values = each value appears in few row 
  groups. Zone maps are most effective here because min/max can exclude most row
  groups. If user_id = 'usr_abc123' and row group N has min=usr_m, max=usr_z,
  it's skipped.

  Low cardinality = few distinct values = each value appears in every row group.
  Zone maps are least effective. service = 'api' exists in every row group →
  zone maps never prune anything. (This is exactly why putting low-cardinality
  attributes into the inverted index makes sense, as we discussed earlier.)

  So the situation is actually worse than I described:

  ┌────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │        Column type         │ Zone map effectiveness  │ Impact of losing │
  │                            │                         │     zone maps    │
  ├────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ High-cardinality (user_id, │ Very effective — skip   │ Big loss         │
  │  trace_id)                 │ most row groups         │                  │
  ├────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Medium-cardinality         │ Moderately effective    │ Moderate loss    │
  │ (endpoint, host)           │                         │                  │
  ├────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Low-cardinality (service,  │ Useless — value in      │ No loss          │
  │ level)                     │ every row group         │                  │
  └────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┴──────────────────┘

  The user_id = 'usr_abc123' case is exactly where zone map loss hurts most. The
  inverted index narrows to 25K rows by text content, but without zone maps on
  user_id, we fetch all 25K and post-filter. With zone maps, we'd fetch maybe
  200 rows from the 1-2 row groups that actually contain that user_id.

  This strengthens the case for Option 2 (apply zone maps ourselves inside the
  table function).

❯ But zone maps are only beneficial when the values can be ordered, right?      

⏺ Yes. Zone maps store [min, max] per row group. Only useful if the data type
  has a total order and the filter is a range/equality check that can be
  compared against min/max.

  Works well:
  - Integers, timestamps, dates — natural order
  - Strings — lexicographic order. user_id = 'usr_abc123' → check if min <= 
  'usr_abc123' <= max
  - The key requirement: values are clustered within row groups. If data is
  inserted in user_id order (or correlated with it), each row group covers a
  narrow range → zone maps prune aggressively.

  Works poorly even with order:
  - If user_id values are randomly distributed across rows (e.g., round-robin
  assignment), every row group spans [usr_aaa..., usr_zzz...] → zone maps never
  prune. The range covers everything.

  Doesn't work at all:
  - Columns where order is meaningless (JSON blobs, arrays, complex types)
  - LIKE '%pattern%' — not a range check

  So the real question is: are log rows ordered by the filter column?

  ┌───────────┬───────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │  Column   │       Ordered in logs?        │      Zone maps useful?      │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ timestamp │ Yes — logs arrive in time     │ Very useful                 │
  │           │ order                         │                             │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ user_id   │ No — users are interleaved    │ Min/max spans everything →  │
  │           │                               │ useless                     │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ trace_id  │ No — traces are interleaved   │ Same — useless              │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ service   │ No — all services emit        │ Same                        │
  │           │ concurrently                  │                             │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ level     │ No — errors mixed with info   │ Same                        │
  └───────────┴───────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  For log data, only timestamp benefits from zone maps in the typical case. All
  other columns have values scattered across all row groups, so [min, max]
  covers the entire value range.

  This changes the calculus:

  - Losing zone map pushdown for user_id, service, level on log tables costs
  almost nothing because those zone maps don't prune anyway
  - Losing zone map pushdown for timestamp would hurt — but we already handle
  timestamp at the index level (partition pruning)
  - The inverted index is actually more effective than zone maps for these
  scattered columns because it has per-row granularity, not per-row-group

  So Option 2 (applying zone maps ourselves) has less value for log workloads
  than I thought. The inverted index with multi-column support (putting service,
  user_id into the index) would be the real fix — giving per-row filtering that
  zone maps can't provide.

❯ The rows are inserted in timestamp order, so not ordered by another attribute 

⏺ Exactly. So for log tables:

  - Zone maps on non-timestamp columns are essentially useless — every row group
  spans the full value range of user_id, service, etc.
  - We're not losing anything by replacing the table scan with our index scan
  function
  - The inverted index IS the pruning mechanism for these columns — it gives
  row-level granularity where zone maps give nothing

  This means Option 2 (applying zone maps ourselves) isn't worth implementing
  for the log use case. The right investment is multi-column indexing — putting
  user_id, service, level into the inverted index alongside message.

  Then:
  WHERE contains(message, 'timeout') AND user_id = 'usr_abc123'

  Becomes: intersect posting lists for message:timeout and user_id:usr_abc123.
  Result: maybe 3 rows, fetched directly. No zone maps needed.

❯ Okay, so table function without pushing down filter is good?                  

⏺ Yes, for log workloads it's fine. The filters that matter are:

  1. Text predicates (contains, prefix) → handled by the inverted index
  2. Timestamp range → handled by partition pruning in the index
  3. Attribute equality (user_id = X, service = Y) → zone maps wouldn't help
  anyway (random distribution). Future fix: multi-column index, not filter
  pushdown.

  The remaining FILTER above the scan does post-filtering on the already-small
  result set from the index. Applying user_id = 'usr_abc123' on 25K rows is
  cheap — it's a string comparison, not I/O.

  The only case where filter pushdown into the scan would matter is if the table
  is not log data (e.g., sorted by user_id). But that's not our target use
  case.

❯ We probably want to push some low-cardinality attributes down to the index    
  (env, service, region).                                                       
  But coming back to the bind data: What do you want to store in the bind data  
  when switching to the approach where the row ids are calculated at execution  
  time?                                                                         

⏺ Store search parameters + how to find the index:

  struct LogsearchScanBindData : public TableFunctionData {
      // Table location (to look up DataTable at execution time)
      string catalog_name;
      string schema_name;
      string table_name;

      // Index location (to look up LogsearchIndex at execution time)
      string index_name;

      // Search parameters (from optimizer analysis)
      vector<string> search_terms;    // for token search (AND semantics)
      string search_prefix;           // for prefix search
      bool is_prefix = false;

      // Timestamp bounds (extracted from co-occurring filters)
      timestamp_t ts_min;
      timestamp_t ts_max;
      bool has_ts_bounds = false;

      // Column info (copied from original LogicalGet)
      vector<LogicalType> all_types;
      vector<string> all_names;
  };

  At execution time (in InitGlobal):

  // 1. Look up the table
  auto &table =
  catalog.GetEntry<TableCatalogEntry>(...).Cast<DuckTableEntry>().GetStorage();

  // 2. Look up the index by name from the table's index list
  auto &index_list = table.GetDataTableInfo()->GetIndexes();
  LogsearchIndex *index = /* find by index_name */;

  // 3. Query the index NOW — fresh data, correct transaction
  auto row_ids = index->SearchTerms(bind_data.search_terms,
                                     bind_data.ts_min, bind_data.ts_max,
                                     bind_data.has_ts_bounds);

  // 4. Store for scan function to consume
  gstate->row_ids = std::move(row_ids);

  What the optimizer does (lightweight):
  1. Find the index, check selectivity → decide whether to use it
  2. Store search parameters + index name in bind data
  3. Replace LogicalGet with our table function

  What execution does (the actual work):
  1. Look up index by name
  2. Query posting lists
  3. Fetch matching rows

  Index lookup by name is safe against drops — if index was dropped between plan
  and execution, we get an error or fall back. No dangling pointer.

❯ Do the change to switch the index lookup to execution phase. The bind data    
  should not contain any pointers because it has to get serialized and          
  transferred via the network.                                                  

  Read 3 files (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Write(extension/logsearch/include/logsearch_index_scan.hpp)
  ⎿  Added 23 lines, removed 7 lines
       7  
       8  #pragma once
       9  
      10 +#include "duckdb/common/types/timestamp.hpp"                   
      11  #include "duckdb/function/table_function.hpp"
      12  
      13  namespace duckdb {
     ...
      16  //! Used by the logsearch optimizer to replace full table scans
           with
      17  //! targeted row fetches based on inverted index results.
      18  //!
      18 -//! Row groups that contain no matching row IDs are never touch
         -ed,                                                            
      19 -//! because DataTable::Fetch() routes directly to the row group
      20 -//! containing each row ID.                                    
      19 +//! The index is queried at execution time (in InitGlobal), not
         + at plan time.                                                 
      20 +//! This ensures fresh data and avoids materializing row IDs du
         +ring planning.                                                 
      21 +//! Row groups that contain no matching row IDs are never touch
         +ed.                                                            
      22  struct LogsearchIndexScan {
      23    static TableFunction GetFunction();
      24  };
      25

⏺ Working. Bind data now contains only serializable search parameters. Index
  queried at execution time.

  What changed:
  - Bind data: row_ids → index_name + search_terms + search_prefix +
  ts_min/ts_max + estimated_count. No pointers, no materialized data.
  - Optimizer: Checks selectivity, estimates count, stores search parameters.
  Does NOT query the index.
  - InitGlobal (execution time): Looks up index by name from table's index list,
  queries posting lists, stores row IDs in global state.

  filter ensures correctness.

✻ Baked for 6m 14s

❯ Summarize your changes from the last commit plus the reasoning behind them. I 
  want to feed them to another Claude that can then integrate the changes with  
  other changes.                                                                

⏺ Summary of changes since WIP commit (3012bcf708)

  Problem

  The initial WIP had a working inverted index extension, but the optimizer rule
  and query execution had critical issues that made the index either unused or
  inefficient.

  Changes and reasoning

  ---
  1. Switch from optimize_function to pre_optimize_function

  File: logsearch_extension.cpp

  Problem: The optimizer rule never fired. DuckDB's filter pushdown (a built-in
  optimizer) moves WHERE contains(msg, 'x') from LogicalFilter into
  LogicalGet.table_filters before extension optimizer hooks run. By the time
  optimize_function ran, there was no LogicalFilter node to match.

  Fix: Register as pre_optimize_function which runs before built-in optimizers.
  The LogicalFilter node still exists at that point.

  ---
  2. Flatten AND conjunctions in filter matching

  File: logsearch_optimizer.cpp

  Problem: The binder produces WHERE contains(msg, 'x') AND ts > y as a single
  CONJUNCTION_AND expression, not two separate filter expressions. The optimizer
  only checked top-level expressions in filter.expressions, never looking
  inside conjunctions.

  Fix: Flatten conjunctions into leaf expressions before matching:
  for (auto &expr : filter.expressions) {
      if (expr->type == CONJUNCTION_AND) {
          for (auto &child : conj.children) leaf_exprs.push_back(child.get());
      } else {
          leaf_exprs.push_back(expr.get());
      }
  }
  Both text predicate matching and timestamp extraction now iterate over
  leaf_exprs.

  ---
  3. Handle CAST-wrapped constants in timestamp extraction

  File: logsearch_optimizer.cpp

  Problem: ts > '2024-01-04' becomes ts > CAST('2024-01-04' AS TIMESTAMP) in the
  bound plan. The right side is a BoundCastExpression, not
  BoundConstantExpression. Old code checked expr.type == VALUE_CONSTANT — always
  false.

  Fix: Use IsFoldable() to detect any expression evaluable at plan time, then
  ExpressionExecutor::TryEvaluateScalar() to resolve it to a concrete Value.
  Handles CAST, implicit casts, constant expressions, etc.

  ---
  4. Fix column name resolution for timestamp column

  File: logsearch_optimizer.cpp

  Problem: Used get.names[binding_col_idx] to check if a column is the timestamp
  column. But binding_col_idx is the position in the projected column list,
  while names is indexed by physical table column index. These differ when not
  all columns are projected.

  Fix: Resolve through column_ids first:
  auto table_col_idx = col_ids[binding_col_idx].GetPrimaryIndex();
  get.names[table_col_idx]  // actual column name

  ---
  5. Replace MARK join with custom table function (index scan)

  Files: New logsearch_index_scan.hpp, logsearch_index_scan.cpp. Modified
  logsearch_optimizer.cpp.

  Problem: The MARK join approach (TABLE_SCAN → HASH_JOIN(MARK) → FILTER) read
  ALL rows from the table. The join only added a boolean column; it didn't
  reduce the scan. For 10M rows with 9K matches, all 10M rows were scanned.

  Fix: Replace the LogicalGet node entirely with a custom table function
  LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN that calls DataTable::Fetch() with only the matching row
  IDs. Row groups containing no matching row IDs are never touched.

  The optimizer preserves the same table_index so all column bindings in the
  plan remain valid:
  auto new_get = make_uniq<LogicalGet>(
      get.table_index,       // same — bindings stay valid
      index_scan_func,       // our function
      bind_data,             // search parameters
      get.returned_types, get.names, get.virtual_columns
  );
  new_get->SetColumnIds(get.GetColumnIds());
  new_get->projection_ids = get.projection_ids;
  filter.children[0] = std::move(new_get);

  Original filter expressions are kept above for correctness (the index may
  return superset results — e.g., tokenized contains is wider than substring
  contains).

  ---
  6. Move index query from plan time to execution time

  Files: logsearch_index_scan.hpp, logsearch_index_scan.cpp,
  logsearch_optimizer.cpp

  Problem: The optimizer materialized all matching row IDs during planning. This
  used memory proportional to result set, could be stale if data changes, and
  the bind data contained pointers/non-serializable data (can't be sent over the
  network for distributed execution).

  Fix: Bind data now stores only serializable search parameters:
  struct LogsearchScanBindData {
      string catalog_name, schema_name, table_name;  // table location
      string index_name;                              // index lookup by name
      vector<string> search_terms;                    // what to search
      string search_prefix;
      bool is_prefix;
      timestamp_t ts_min, ts_max;                     // timestamp bounds
      bool has_ts_bounds;
      idx_t estimated_count;                          // for cardinality 
  estimation
  };

  The actual index query happens in InitGlobal (execution time):
  1. Look up table by name → DataTable
  2. Look up index by name from table's index list → LogsearchIndex
  3. Query posting lists with search parameters → vector<row_t>
  4. Fetch only those rows during scan

  If the index was dropped between plan and execution, result is empty. The
  original filter above ensures correctness (falls through to checking every row
  that passes).

  ---
  7. Implement TryDelete and VerifyBuffers on LogsearchIndex

  File: logsearch_index.cpp, logsearch_index.hpp

  Problem: INSERT INTO a table with a logsearch index crashed with "TryDelete
  not implemented" during transaction rollback. DuckDB's default
  BoundIndex::TryDelete and VerifyBuffers throw exceptions.

  Fix: Implement as no-ops. TryDelete returns 0 (stale entries remain but
  post-filter ensures correctness). VerifyBuffers has nothing to verify. For
  append-only log tables, deletes are rare/irrelevant.

  ---
  8. Tests

  New files: 5 test files, 141 assertions total.

  - test_logsearch_basic.test — Core functionality: create, query, drop
  - test_logsearch_plan_validation.test — EXPLAIN checks: verify
  LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN appears for selective queries, doesn't appear for common
  terms
  - test_logsearch_correctness.test — EXCEPT queries comparing indexed results
  to brute-force results. Both directions (indexed minus expected = 0, expected
  minus indexed = 0)
  - test_logsearch_edge_cases.test — Single row, all NULL, empty strings, long
  messages, special characters, insert after index, multiple indexes, case
  sensitivity
  - test_logsearch_projections.test — Column subsets, SELECT *, aggregations,
  ORDER BY, LIMIT, non-indexed filter combination

  ---
  Architecture after changes

  Query: SELECT * FROM logs WHERE contains(message, 'error') AND ts >
  '2024-01-04'

  Optimizer (pre_optimize):
    1. Flatten AND → leaf exprs: [contains(msg,'error'), ts > CAST(...)]
    2. Match contains → find logsearch index on msg column
    3. Extract ts bounds from ts > comparison (via TryEvaluateScalar)
    4. Check selectivity of 'error' → 10% < 20% threshold → use index
    5. Store search params in bind data (no row IDs, no pointers)
    6. Replace LogicalGet with LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN

  Execution (InitGlobal):
    1. Look up table + index by name
    2. Query inverted index: SearchTerms(["error"], ts_min, ts_max)
    3. Index skips partitions where ts_max < ts_min → returns subset of row IDs

  Execution (Scan):
    1. DataTable::Fetch() with batches of row IDs
    2. Only touches row groups containing matching IDs
    3. Post-filter applies original contains() + ts > for correctness

  Plan shape:
    AGGREGATE → FILTER [contains(msg,'error') AND ts > ...] →
  LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN [~25K rows]

  Key files

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │                     File                     │           Role            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │                                              │ Entry point, registers    │
  │ extension/logsearch/logsearch_extension.cpp  │ IndexType + pre_optimize  │
  │                                              │ hook                      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │                                              │ PostingList,              │
  │ extension/logsearch/logsearch_index.cpp      │ IndexPartition,           │
  │                                              │ LogsearchIndex            │
  │                                              │ (BoundIndex)              │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │                                              │ Table function: fetches   │
  │ extension/logsearch/logsearch_index_scan.cpp │ rows by ID via            │
  │                                              │ DataTable::Fetch()        │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ extension/logsearch/logsearch_build.cpp      │ IndexType build callbacks │
  │                                              │  (CREATE INDEX path)      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ extension/logsearch/logsearch_optimizer.cpp  │ Pre-optimize rule: match  │
  │                                              │ filters, replace scan     │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │                                              │ Split on non-alpha,       │
  │ extension/logsearch/logsearch_tokenizer.cpp  │ lowercase, skip           │
  │                                              │ single-char               │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ extension/extension_config_local.cmake       │ Enables the extension in  │
  │                                              │ local builds              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

✻ Churned for 1m 1s

❯ While another Claude is working on the implementation, what do you think are  
  open questions still?                                                         

⏺ Open questions

  Correctness

  1. contains() rewrite is semantically wider than the function. contains(msg, 
  'on tim') → tokenizer produces ["tim"] → index returns all rows with "tim"
  anywhere. Post-filter catches it, but the index scan fetches way more rows
  than needed. Should we only rewrite when the search string consists of whole
  tokens? Or accept the over-fetch since the alternative is a full scan anyway?
  2. Insert-after-index correctness. New rows appended after CREATE INDEX get
  added via BoundIndex::Append, which creates single-row partitions with broken
  structure. The post-filter saves correctness (new rows pass through without
  index acceleration), but the index degrades silently. Should we fall back to
  full scan when the index has too many micro-partitions? Or rebuild affected
  partitions?
  3. Concurrent modification during execution. The index is queried in
  InitGlobal (start of execution). If another transaction commits inserts
  between InitGlobal and the actual Fetch() calls, the row IDs from the index
  are stale. Post-filter is correct for false positives, but we could miss newly
  inserted rows entirely. Is this acceptable for log workloads (append-only,
  eventual consistency)?
  4. Transaction isolation. DataTable::Fetch() respects MVCC — it only returns
  rows visible to the current transaction. But the inverted index itself has no
  MVCC awareness. Row IDs in the index might reference rows that were deleted in
  the current transaction's view. Post-filter handles this (deleted rows won't
  be returned by Fetch), but is there a case where this breaks?

  Performance

  5. No LIMIT pushdown. SELECT * FROM logs WHERE contains(msg, 'error') ORDER BY
  ts DESC LIMIT 10 queries the entire index, fetches all matching rows, sorts,
  then takes 10. Should iterate partitions newest-first and stop after finding
  enough rows. How to communicate the limit from the execution engine to our
  table function? TableFunctionInitInput doesn't seem to carry limit info.
  6. Cardinality estimation is a guess. We estimate min(df_per_term) at
  optimizer time. The actual row count at execution time could differ (new
  data). Does the optimizer use our estimate for downstream decisions (join
  ordering, memory allocation)? Bad estimates could cause poor physical plans.
  7. Per-row Fetch overhead. DataTable::Fetch() does per-row routing to the
  correct row group. For 100K matching rows, that's 100K individual lookups. A
  batch-per-row-group approach (group row IDs by row group, scan each row group
  once with a selection vector) would be faster. Worth implementing?
  8. Index build is single-pass. build_sink tokenizes and builds posting lists
  in one pass. For 10M rows, the entire inverted index is in memory during
  build. Should we spill partitions to disk during build for very large tables?

  Design

  9. Multi-column indexing. We discussed indexing service, level alongside
  message. Key prefix approach ("service:api", "message:error"). But the
  optimizer needs to recognize WHERE service = 'api' as indexable — currently it
  only matches contains() and prefix(). Exact equality on non-text columns is a
  different pattern. How to unify?
  10. Tokenizer is too simplistic. Splits 192.168.1.100 into ["192", "168", 
  "100"]. Can't search for an IP address as a unit. Same for UUIDs, URLs, dotted
  paths. Need configurable tokenization — but how? Per-column tokenizer config?
  WITH (tokenizer = 'log_aware')?
  11. No persistence. Index is lost on database close/checkpoint. What
  serialization format? Per-partition files? DuckDB's block manager? Custom
  format? How to handle checkpoint — serialize on checkpoint, deserialize on
  load?
  12. col = 'value' not intercepted. Equality filters get pushed into table scan
  filters during binding, before any optimizer hook. Three options: (a) custom
  TableFilter subclass, (b) replacement scan, (c) intercept at physical plan
  level. Which is least invasive?
  13. Local storage gap. ART index scan has a second phase scanning uncommitted
  local inserts. Our table function doesn't see local storage at all. For a
  single-user embedded database this might not matter (inserts are committed
  before queries in typical log pipelines). For concurrent workloads it's a gap.
  14. Index name conflicts. If user creates CREATE INDEX idx ON t1 USING 
  logsearch(msg) and CREATE INDEX idx ON t2 USING logsearch(msg) — we look up by
  index name. DuckDB prevents same-name indexes in same schema, so this is
  probably fine. But worth verifying.

  Testing

  15. No performance regression tests. We verify correctness but not that the
  index actually speeds things up. A test that checks EXPLAIN ANALYZE row counts
  (LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN scans fewer rows than table total) would catch
  regressions where the optimizer stops using the index.
  16. No concurrent access tests. Multiple queries hitting the same index
  simultaneously. Multiple inserts while queries are running. Drop index while
  query is executing.
  17. No large-scale tests. Current tests use 10K-50K rows (1 row group). Need
  tests with multiple row groups (>122K rows) to verify partition-level behavior
  and timestamp pruning actually skips partitions.

✻ Cooked for 45s

❯ If you would start again with implementing the extension, with which part     
  would you start?                                                              
⏺ Start with the harness, not the data structures.

  Order:

  1. Extension scaffold + IndexType registration. Empty BoundIndex subclass.
  CREATE INDEX ... USING logsearch(msg) parses + persists in catalog. No actual
  indexing yet.
  2. Custom table function for row-ID fetch. LOGSEARCH_INDEX_SCAN that takes
  hardcoded row IDs in bind data, calls DataTable::Fetch(). Verify plan
  replacement works. Verify row group skipping via EXPLAIN ANALYZE.
  3. Optimizer rule wiring. pre_optimize_function, flatten conjunctions, match
  contains(col, 'x'), replace LogicalGet. Hardcode row IDs [0, 100, 200].
  Confirm bindings stay valid, projections work, post-filter kept.
  4. Tokenizer. Trivial: split alnum, lowercase. Tests for special chars, NULL,
  empty.
  5. PostingList + IndexPartition. Sorted vector<uint32_t>, std::map dict. Unit
  tests for intersect/union/prefix.
  6. Build callbacks. build_sink → tokenize chunks → fill partitions. Skip
  multi-thread build_combine first pass (single-thread sink).
  7. Wire optimizer to index. Replace hardcoded row IDs with
  index->SearchTerms(...).
  8. Timestamp pruning. Zone maps in build_global_init via GetPartitionStats.
  Extract bounds from filters.
  9. Selectivity threshold.
  10. Tests at every step.

  Why this order

  Harness first = catch integration bugs early. Plan replacement, binding
  preservation, post-filter — these broke first time around. Don't want index
  built before discovering optimizer hook fires too late (optimize_function vs
  pre_optimize_function bug).

  Hardcoded row IDs = isolate variables. When index returns wrong results, was
  it tokenizer? Posting list? Optimizer? Hardcoding eliminates upstream
  uncertainty.

  Table function before BoundIndex internals. Confirms DataTable::Fetch() path
  works for our use case before sinking weeks into index data structures.

  What different second time

  - Test EXPLAIN plan shape from query 1. First time I wrote correctness tests,
  didn't notice plans were full scans. Plan validation tests should come first.
  - Skip MARK join entirely. Dead end. Go straight to table function
  replacement.
  - pre_optimize_function from start. Filter pushdown bug cost a debug cycle.
  - Serializable bind data from start. No pointers, no materialized row IDs.
  Index query at execution time.
  - Don't implement features tests don't cover. No exact-match rewrite, no
  prefix, no compound terms until basic contains() solid end-to-end.

✻ Brewed for 47s

※ recap: Building DuckDB logsearch extension (inverted index for log full-text search); MVP works with index scan replacing table scan, 141 tests pass. Next: address open questions, top priority is LIMIT pushdown for ORDER BY ts DESC LIMIT N queries. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ Is it recommended for extensions to use DuckDB's memory allocator? Otherwise, 
  the memory used by the extension would be untracked, right? How? What would   
  be a good abstraction to use to shield myself from changes of DuckDB          
  internals?                                                                    

⏺ Yes, use DuckDB allocator. Untracked memory = no eviction under pressure, no
  accounting in pragma memory_usage, no buffer pool limits respected. DuckDB can
  OOM while extension hogs memory.

  How

  Three tiers, increasing integration:

  Tier 1: Allocator::Get(db) — easiest

  auto &allocator = Allocator::Get(db);
  auto *ptr = allocator.AllocateData(size);
  // ... use ...
  allocator.FreeData(ptr, size);

  - Goes through BufferManager accounting
  - Counts toward memory_limit
  - No eviction (memory pinned forever)
  - Use for: small bookkeeping, dictionary nodes, metadata

  Tier 2: BufferManager::Allocate(MemoryTag, size) — evictable

  auto &buffer_manager = BufferManager::GetBufferManager(db);
  auto handle = buffer_manager.Allocate(MemoryTag::EXTENSION, size);
  auto *ptr = handle.Ptr();

  - Returns BufferHandle — RAII
  - Can be unpinned → evictable to disk
  - Use for: posting lists, dictionary blocks (cold partitions)

  Tier 3: FixedSizeAllocator — ART pattern

  FixedSizeAllocator allocator(node_size, buffer_manager);
  auto pointer = allocator.New();  // returns IndexPointer (8 bytes)
  auto *node = allocator.Get<Node>(pointer);

  - Block-based, fixed-size slots
  - Best for many small uniform allocations (tree nodes)
  - Serializable
  - Use for: dictionary tree nodes if you go ART-style for terms

  Abstraction

  Don't depend on Allocator / BufferManager types directly throughout code.
  Wrap.

  // logsearch_memory.hpp
  namespace logsearch {

  class MemoryManager {
  public:
      explicit MemoryManager(duckdb::DatabaseInstance &db);

      // Hot path — small allocations, tracked, pinned
      void *Allocate(size_t bytes);
      void Free(void *ptr, size_t bytes);

      // Cold path — large blocks, evictable
      class Block {
      public:
          uint8_t *data();
          size_t size() const;
      };
      std::unique_ptr<Block> AllocateBlock(size_t bytes);

      // STL-compatible allocator for std::vector<T, LogsearchAllocator<T>>
      template <typename T>
      LogsearchAllocator<T> GetStlAllocator();

  private:
      duckdb::Allocator &small_allocator_;       // Tier 1
      duckdb::BufferManager &buffer_manager_;    // Tier 2
  };

  } // namespace logsearch

  Then PostingList, IndexPartition take MemoryManager &. DuckDB internals
  isolated to single file.

  STL allocator

  vector<uint32_t> posting list → use custom allocator that routes through
  DuckDB:

  template <typename T>
  class LogsearchAllocator {
  public:
      using value_type = T;
      T *allocate(size_t n) { return static_cast<T*>(mm_.Allocate(n *
  sizeof(T))); }
      void deallocate(T *p, size_t n) { mm_.Free(p, n * sizeof(T)); }
  private:
      MemoryManager &mm_;
  };

  using PostingListVec = std::vector<uint32_t, LogsearchAllocator<uint32_t>>;

  Standard vector<uint32_t> uses malloc → untracked. Custom allocator = tracked,
  drop-in replacement.

  What changes you shield against

  - Allocator API churn (rare but happens)
  - Switch from Allocator to FixedSizeAllocator later — only MemoryManager impl
  changes
  - Add eviction tracking — add hook in MemoryManager
  - Add metrics/profiling — central choke point

  Caveats

  - DuckDB Allocator doesn't free across thread boundaries cheaply. Per-thread
  arena if hot.
  - BufferManager::Allocate overhead per call. Batch large allocs.
  - MemoryTag needs adding to enum upstream for clean accounting. Use existing
  tag (e.g. EXTENSION) for now.

  Recommendation for logsearch

  Phase 1: Allocator::Get(db) + STL allocator wrapper for posting lists. Done in
  a day.
  Phase 2: When persistence lands, BufferManager::Allocate for cold partition
  blocks. Evictable.
  Phase 3: FixedSizeAllocator if dictionary becomes hot path bottleneck.

  Don't skip Phase 1. Untracked memory = production hazard.

✻ Churned for 58s

❯ What kind of objects will I have to allocate? Temporary objects during index  
  construction and query processing. Dictionary. Let's think about how we       
  should structure this to make it possible to not need the entire dictionary   
  in memory the whole time, but make it spillable. What are your ideas? How     
  would this be serialized to disk? What type of allocator does this require?   
  For the posting lists, they can get very large. At some point, they might     
  store document IDs, positional information and more. How would a good         
  structure for this look like in memory and serialized? Propose for dictionary 
  and posting lists different options and compare them. What else am I          
  missing?                                                                      

⏺ Objects to allocate

  Build time:
  - Tokenized strings (transient — drop after partition flush)
  - Per-thread local hash maps term → row_ids (transient)
  - Arena per chunk for token strings (transient)
  - Per-partition dictionary (persistent until flush)
  - Per-partition posting lists (persistent until flush)
  - Row group boundary metadata (small, persistent)

  Query time:
  - Result row ID vector (transient, per query)
  - Intermediate intersection/union buffers (transient)
  - Decompressed posting list segments (cache-sized)
  - Dictionary block reads (loaded on demand)

  Index lifetime:
  - Partition metadata (small, always in memory)
  - Hot dictionary blocks (LRU cached)
  - Hot posting list blocks (LRU cached)

  Dictionary — spillable design options

  Option A: Sorted string table (SSTable-like)

  On disk per partition:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Header: term_count, index_offset    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Data blocks (4KB each, compressed): │
  │   block 0: ["apple", "banana", ...] │
  │   block 1: ["cherry", "date", ...]  │
  │   ...                               │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Sparse index (in memory):           │
  │   ("apple",   block_0_offset)       │
  │   ("cherry",  block_1_offset)       │
  │   ("kiwi",    block_2_offset)       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┘

  - Front-coding within blocks (shared prefix). 5-10x compression typical.
  - Lookup: binary search sparse index → load block → binary search block.
  - Memory: sparse index in RAM (1 entry per block, ~0.1% overhead).
  - Spill: blocks live on disk, loaded via BufferManager::Pin/Unpin.
  - Good for: prefix scan (sequential block read), point lookup.
  - Bad for: random updates (immutable).

  Option B: FST (Finite State Transducer) — Tantivy/Lucene approach

  - Compressed automaton mapping term → posting list offset
  - 10-50x compression for log data (high prefix overlap)
  - Mmap-friendly, lookup without decompression
  - Memory: tiny (FST stays mmap'd, OS pages it)
  - Bad: complex to implement, no incremental updates
  - Library: fst-rs exists but C++ libs scarce

  Option C: B-tree blocks

  - Internal nodes mostly in memory, leaves spillable
  - Logarithmic lookup
  - Allows updates (split/merge)
  - Heavier per-node overhead than SSTable
  - Good if mutation needed; we're append-only, so wasted complexity

  Option D: Hash table sharded by hash prefix

  - Constant-time lookup
  - No prefix scan (kills LIKE 'prefix%' acceleration)
  - Bad fit for our needs

  Recommendation

  SSTable (Option A) for v1. Simple, fits append-only + immutable partition
  model. Prefix scan works. Front-coding gives big wins for log data (lots of
  shared prefixes). FST (Option B) is the long-term win but defer.

  Posting list options

  Option A: Sorted uint32_t[]

  - Current. Simple, sorted, fast intersection.
  - 4 bytes per entry. 1M entries = 4MB.
  - Bad: no compression, no skip, no extensions (positions etc).
  - Good for very small lists (<128 entries).

  Option B: Delta + varint encoding

  [100, 105, 200, 250]
    → deltas [100, 5, 95, 50]
    → varint [0x64, 0x05, 0x5F, 0x32]

  - 1-2 bytes per entry typical. 4-10x smaller.
  - Sequential decode only. Bad for random access.
  - Add skip table every N entries → ~16 byte overhead per 128 entries.
  - Good for medium-large lists.

  Option C: Roaring bitmap

  Chunk by upper 16 bits of row ID:
    Chunk dense (≥4096): bitset (8KB)
    Chunk sparse (<4096): sorted uint16[]
    Chunk full: run-length

  - Best general-purpose. 3-10x smaller than uint32[] for typical distributions.
  - O(1) AND/OR via bitwise ops on dense chunks.
  - O(1) NOT (cheap complement on bitsets).
  - Per-chunk parallelism natural.
  - Library: CRoaring (C, MIT, mature, single-header available).
  - Cost: more code, dependency.

  Option D: PFOR-delta (Patched Frame-Of-Reference)

  - Used by Elasticsearch/Lucene posting lists
  - Block-based, bit-packs deltas, patches outliers separately
  - Faster decode than varint (SIMD-friendly)
  - Compression similar to roaring for sparse, worse for dense
  - Complex to implement

  With positional info

  Three streams per term:
  Posting list:  [doc_id_1, doc_id_2, ...]
  Frequency:     [freq_1, freq_2, ...]      (positions per doc)
  Positions:     [pos_1_1, pos_1_2, ..., pos_2_1, ...]

  Lucene does this. Frequency stream tells you how many positions belong to each
  doc. Stored as 3 separate compressed blocks.

  For our use case: skip positions in v1. Add when phrase queries needed.
  Reserve format extensibility upfront (block header indicates what streams
  present).

  Recommendation

  Tiered:
  - Small lists (<128 entries): inline sorted uint16[] in dictionary entry. No
  separate file.
  - Medium-large: roaring bitmap. CRoaring vendored.
  - Positional data: separate parallel stream when needed. Defer to v2.

  Reasoning: roaring is industry-standard, well-tested, and gives best ops
  across density spectrum. Inline for small saves indirection.

  On-disk layout per partition

  partition_<row_start>.lsi (logsearch index):

  ┌────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Magic + version (8 bytes)      │
  ├────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Footer offset (8 bytes)        │ — written last, read first
  ├────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Dictionary blocks              │
  │   block 0 (front-coded)        │
  │   block 1                      │
  │   ...                          │
  ├────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Posting list blocks            │
  │   roaring container 0          │
  │   roaring container 1          │
  │   ...                          │
  ├────────────────────────────────┤
  │ (Future) position blocks       │
  ├────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Footer:                        │
  │   sparse dict index            │
  │   block offset table           │
  │   row_start, row_count         │
  │   ts_min, ts_max               │
  │   total_terms, total_postings  │
  └────────────────────────────────┘

  Footer at end → write sequentially, seek once to load metadata.

  Each partition = single file. Atomic write (write to .tmp, rename). Simplifies
  crash recovery.

  Allocator types per need

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────┬─────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │      Object      │   Lifetime   │    Size     │        Allocator        │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Build tokens     │ μs (per      │ small, many │ ArenaAllocator (reset   │
  │                  │ chunk)       │             │ per chunk)              │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Build hash map   │ minutes (per │ small, many │ Allocator::Get(db)      │
  │ nodes            │  build)      │             │                         │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Build posting    │ minutes      │ grows       │ Allocator::Get(db) or   │
  │ list buffers     │              │             │ unsafe_vector           │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Hot dict block   │ LRU lifetime │ 4KB         │ BufferManager pinned    │
  │ (loaded)         │              │             │ BufferHandle            │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Cold dict block  │ on-disk      │ 4KB         │ BlockManager block      │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Hot posting list │ LRU lifetime │ variable    │ BufferManager pinned    │
  │                  │              │             │ BufferHandle            │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Sparse dict      │ partition    │ small       │ Allocator::Get(db)      │
  │ index            │ lifetime     │             │                         │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Query result row │ μs (per      │ variable    │ DuckDB execution arena  │
  │  IDs             │ query)       │             │                         │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Intersection     │ per query    │ 2x posting  │ thread-local arena      │
  │ buffers          │              │ list size   │                         │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────┴─────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  Key abstractions:

  - Block cache — LRUCache<BlockId, BufferHandle> over BufferManager. Pin on
  hit, evict LRU on miss.
  - Mmap layer — if you want mmap'd FST/SSTable instead. DuckDB has primitives
  via FileSystem.
  - Arena per partition build — destroy in one shot when partition flushed.

  What you're missing

  1. Block size choice. 4KB matches OS page. 64KB amortizes seek but worse
  cache. DuckDB defaults to 256KB blocks. Test.
  2. Compression of dict block content. Front-coding alone isn't enough. Add LZ4
  on top? Zstd? DuckDB uses FSST for strings — reuse it.
  3. CRC / checksums. Bit rot detection. Per-block CRC32C.
  4. Concurrency on hot partitions. Read-write lock during merge. Or
  copy-on-write — flush replaces partition atomically.
  5. Partition merge / compaction. Many small partitions → query touches many
  files. Periodically merge K small partitions into one. Background.
  6. Index versioning. On-disk format will change. Magic + version in header.
  Plan migration path (or refuse to load old, force rebuild).
  7. Statistics file separate from data. partition_meta.lsi with just zone maps
  + counts. Loaded eagerly. Data files loaded on demand.
  8. Doc-frequency in dict entry. Stored next to term, not in posting list.
  Cheap selectivity check without loading posting list.
  9. Bloom filter per partition. Tiny, in-memory, says "term definitely not in
  this partition." Skip dict lookup entirely for misses.
  10. Term ID assignment. Within partition, terms get sequential IDs. Posting
  lists reference term ID, not string. Saves space when posting list points to
  dictionary.
  11. Memory limits during build. Spill local thread state if it exceeds
  threshold. Multi-pass build for huge partitions.
  12. WAL integration. If DuckDB crashes mid-build, what happens? Rebuild from
  scratch? Per-partition build is naturally restartable.
  13. Endianness / portability. Define byte order. Use varint for portability.
  14. Test for unaligned access. uint16 arrays in roaring need alignment care on
  ARM.
  15. Skip lists in posting lists. Galloping intersection needs skip pointers
  every N entries. CRoaring handles, but custom format needs it.
  16. String storage in dict. Inline or separate strings region? Front-coding
  gives inline. Variable-length strings interleaved with offsets.
  17. Sharing strings across partitions. Same term "error" in 100 partitions =
  string stored 100 times. Could deduplicate via global string pool. Probably
  not worth it — front-coding already cheap.
  18. Position encoding format. When positions land: per-doc gap-coded positions
  in own stream. Skip pointer aligned with doc stream.

  Concrete recommendation

  namespace logsearch {

  // Compact dictionary entry on disk
  struct DictEntry {
      uint16_t term_offset;       // into block string buffer
      uint16_t term_length;
      uint32_t doc_frequency;     // for selectivity
      uint64_t posting_list_ptr;  // offset in posting file, OR
                                  // inline data if doc_frequency < 
  INLINE_THRESHOLD
  };

  // In-memory partition handle
  class IndexPartition {
      // Always resident
      PartitionMetadata meta;          // ts_min/max, row_start/count
      SparseDictIndex sparse_index;    // 1 entry per dict block
      BloomFilter bloom;               // optional, skip dict lookup

      // Lazy-loaded via BufferManager
      BlockCache<DictBlock> dict_cache;
      BlockCache<PostingBlock> posting_cache;

      // On-disk file (one per partition)
      unique_ptr<FileHandle> data_file;
  };

  // Single allocator entry point
  class MemoryManager {
  public:
      // Small, pinned, tracked
      void *AllocateData(size_t bytes);

      // Block, evictable
      BufferHandle AllocateBlock(MemoryTag tag, size_t bytes);

      // Arena for transient
      ArenaAllocator &GetArena();

      // STL allocator wrapper
      template <typename T> StlAlloc<T> stl();
  };

  } // namespace logsearch

  Build path: posting lists in unsafe_vector<uint32_t> (uses Allocator). On
  flush, encode to roaring + write block file. Drop in-memory vectors.

  Query path: load dict blocks on demand (cache). Load posting blocks on demand
  (cache). Decompress roaring → AND/OR in place → output row IDs.

  Decision points to defer to v2:
  - Positional data
  - FST dictionary
  - Cross-partition dedup
  - Background merge/compaction

  Decision points for v1:
  - SSTable + front-coding
  - Roaring posting lists + inline for tiny
  - BufferManager-backed block cache
  - One file per partition
  - Allocator::Get(db) for everything tracked

✻ Churned for 1m 38s

❯ The posting list also needs to get sorted during new inserts, right?          

⏺ Short answer

  Posting lists must be sorted. But log workload gives you monotonic row IDs for
  free → append-in-order works → no explicit sort.

  Why monotonic holds

  DuckDB row ID assignment:
  - New rows get next available ID, sequentially within row group
  - New row groups append at end → higher row_start than any existing
  - Within a single Append() chunk, row IDs already sorted
  - Across appends, row IDs strictly increase

  → Each posting list receives row IDs in increasing order.

  Cases per access pattern

  Build (build_sink)

  - Table scan returns rows in row ID order
  - Chunks have monotonic row IDs internally
  - Routing by partition → each partition gets its subset, still in order
  - Single thread per local index → trivially sorted

  Just append(). No sort.

  Combine (build_combine)

  - Merge two local posting lists (same partition, different threads)
  - Both already sorted → two-pointer merge, O(n+m)
  - Same algorithm as PostingList::Union

  Append after index exists (BoundIndex::Append)

  - New rows always have IDs > all existing
  - Append to back of posting list, sorted invariant holds
  - D_ASSERT(new_id > back()) catches violations

  Update

  - DuckDB update = delete + insert
  - Inserted row gets new ID at tail → sorted append
  - Deleted row's ID still in posting list (stale) → handled by post-filter on
  Fetch() which respects MVCC

  Compaction / Vacuum

  - DuckDB may reassign row IDs
  - Breaks index entirely. Posting lists reference dead IDs.
  - Solution: rebuild index after compaction, or hook into vacuum lifecycle.
  Currently no support — documented as limitation.

  Sorted vs unsorted structures

  ┌──────────────────────┬────────────────┬─────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │      Structure       │ Out-of-order   │  Sort cost  │     Use when      │
  │                      │     insert     │             │                   │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Sorted               │ requires       │ O(n) if     │ log workload      │
  │ vector<uint32_t>     │ monotonic      │ shift       │ (current)         │
  │ append               │                │             │                   │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Sorted vector +      │ accepts any    │ O(n log n)  │ bulk build w/ no  │
  │ final sort           │ order          │ once        │ ordering          │
  │                      │                │             │ guarantee         │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Roaring bitmap       │ accepts any    │ implicit    │ mixed workload,   │
  │                      │ order          │ per chunk   │ large lists       │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ Skip list            │ accepts any    │ O(log n)    │ many random       │
  │                      │ order          │ per insert  │ inserts           │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────┼─────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ B+ tree              │ accepts any    │ O(log n)    │ mutable, ordered  │
  │                      │ order          │ per insert  │ scan              │
  └──────────────────────┴────────────────┴─────────────┴───────────────────┘

  For log workload: sorted vector + append-only is right. Roaring handles
  unsorted gracefully → safer choice if you can't guarantee monotonic.

  Recommendation

  Three tiers:

  1. Build path: unsafe_vector<uint32_t> with D_ASSERT(new > back()). Fast,
  simple, catches bugs.
  2. Append path (post-build): same — assert monotonic invariant. If violated
  (shouldn't happen in log workload), fall back to insertion sort or refuse.
  3. Hot persistent path: convert to roaring on flush. Roaring is
  order-independent for AND/OR ops. Future-proof against non-monotonic edge
  cases.

  What can break monotonic

  - Parallel inserts via parallel append → DuckDB serializes row ID assignment
  under append lock. Still monotonic globally, but local thread states in
  build_sink might see ranges out of order across chunks.
    - Mitigation: per-partition routing in sink. Each partition processes rows
  in increasing order from its own perspective.
  - Multi-table joins inserting into target → not our concern.
  - WAL replay → rows arrive in commit order, which matches assignment order.
  OK.
  - Restoration from checkpoint → row IDs preserved. Rebuilding index sees same
  order. OK.

  Concrete code shape

  class PostingList {
      void Add(uint32_t row_id) {
          D_ASSERT(data_.empty() || row_id > data_.back());
          data_.push_back(row_id);
      }

      // For roaring backend later:
      void AddBatch(const uint32_t *ids, size_t n) {
          // roaring: roaring_bitmap_add_many — handles unsorted
          // sorted vec: assert monotonic, then memcpy
      }
  };

  Bottom line: yes posting lists must be sorted, but the log workload's natural
  order means you never sort explicitly. Assert the invariant, append fast.

✻ Worked for 1m 15s

❯ What are we optimizing for? Performance (index construction and query speed), 
  can use most of the available memory for index construction, but should work  
  under memory pressure. Disk space is not relevant. What else?                 

⏺ Beyond what you stated

  Latency

  - Query p50 — hot path, common case
  - Query p99 — worst case bounds matter for interactive UI
  - Index build time — affects CREATE INDEX and post-ingest readiness
  - Plan time — optimizer rule runs per query, must be cheap
  - Append latency — index update on INSERT shouldn't stall ingest

  Throughput

  - Build throughput — rows/sec during CREATE INDEX (parallel scan utilization)
  - Query QPS — concurrent queries on same index
  - Ingest throughput — sustained INSERT rate with index maintained
  - Read amplification — bytes touched per matching row

  Recency / access skew

  - Hot partition latency — recent partitions queried 80%+ of time. Optimize
  cache for them.
  - Cold partition latency — old partitions occasionally queried. Acceptable to
  be slower (disk load).
  - Eviction behavior — old partitions evict first under memory pressure.

  Selectivity

  - Needle queries — selective, few matches. Must be fast.
  - Common term fallback — high-selectivity term, threshold trips, full scan.
  Should not be slower than baseline.
  - Compound queries — term1 AND term2 AND ts > x. Intersection cost matters.

  Memory behavior

  - Build memory ceiling — can use most of available, but memory_limit must be
  respected
  - Spill threshold — when local thread state exceeds X, spill to disk
  - Cache size adaptation — block cache shrinks under pressure
  - No leaks under cancellation — query cancelled mid-fetch shouldn't leak

  Concurrency

  - Query parallelism — multi-thread scan across partitions
  - Read while write — query during INSERT shouldn't block
  - Build parallelism — N threads filling per-thread local indexes
  - Combine parallelism — merge K local indexes in parallel pairs

  Predictability

  - No performance cliffs — selectivity 19% vs 21% shouldn't differ 100x
  - Stable plan choice — small data changes shouldn't flip index on/off
  - Deterministic results — same query = same rows (transaction-stable)

  Operational

  - Diagnostics — EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows row groups touched, dict blocks loaded,
  posting list bytes read
  - Metrics — cache hit rate, eviction count, build time per partition,
  selectivity rejections
  - Logging hooks — verbose mode for debugging
  - Stats exposure — duckdb_indexes extension to show index size, term count,
  memory usage
  - Failure modes — clear errors when index can't be used; never wrong results

  Crash safety

  - Persistent index survives crash — atomic per-partition writes
  - Build is restartable — partial build state doesn't corrupt
  - WAL coexistence — index update + table write atomic per transaction (if
  persisted)

  API stability

  - Insulation from DuckDB internals — abstraction layer so DuckDB API churn is
  contained
  - Versioned on-disk format — old indexes upgradeable or auto-rebuilt

  Correctness under MVCC

  - Snapshot consistency — query sees index state matching its transaction
  snapshot
  - No phantom rows — concurrent insert visible only after commit
  - Visibility through post-filter — index over-returns is OK, under-returns is
  not

  What you should NOT optimize for (given your constraints)

  - Disk space → you said irrelevant
  - Compression ratio (except as it affects cache hit rate)
  - Memory efficiency at idle (use most of budget)

  Trade-offs to make explicit

  - Build speed vs build memory — bigger thread-local state = faster build, more
  memory. You said use most → bias toward speed.
  - Query speed vs index size — bigger index (less compression) = faster reads.
  Trade for speed.
  - Plan time vs query time — selectivity check at plan time costs μs but saves
  ms of execution. Worth it.
  - Append speed vs query speed — keeping index optimized on every append slows
  ingest. Defer optimization to background.
  - Latency vs throughput — smaller batches lower latency but lower throughput.

  Recommended priorities

  1. Query latency for needle queries (p99)
  2. Build throughput (parallel utilization)
  3. Append latency (don't block ingest)
  4. Memory behavior under pressure (graceful degradation)
  5. Plan time (cheap selectivity check)
  6. Throughput on concurrent queries
  7. Cold start (acceptable to be slow)

  Anti-goals

  - Don't optimize for many-row-update workload (logs are append-only)
  - Don't optimize for analytics over indexed column (use raw scan + zone maps)
  - Don't aim for full SQL compatibility on the index (limited query patterns
  OK)
  - Don't optimize for tiny tables (full scan beats index)

  Hidden requirement: dev velocity

  - Code clarity > micro-optimization
  - Tests cover all paths (index used, fallback, edge cases)
  - Easy to add features (multi-column, positional) without rewrite
  - Failure to use index is fast — don't punish queries that aren't
  index-eligible

  Numbers to set targets

  - Build: > 1M rows/sec/thread for text-only column
  - Query (needle, hot): < 10ms for 100M-row table
  - Query (needle, cold): < 100ms (disk loads)
  - Append: index update < 10% overhead vs no-index insert
  - Plan time: < 100μs for non-eligible queries (cheap rejection)
  - Memory: index size 10-30% of indexed text size (with roaring + front-coding)

✻ Brewed for 58s

❯ Query performance: Should be parallized were possible, SIMD friendly, working 
  for modern hardware, optimizing memory hierarchy access (RAM, cache, SSD)     

⏺ Parallelism

  Partition-level (coarsest, easiest)

  - Each partition independent → fan out across threads
  - DuckDB pipeline has N threads → assign partitions round-robin
  - No locks, no shared state in hot path
  - MaxThreads() in GlobalTableFunctionState → number of partitions to touch
  - Currently we do row-ID-level parallelism (each thread grabs
  STANDARD_VECTOR_SIZE row IDs). Change to partition-level — each thread handles
  one partition end-to-end: query its dict, intersect posting lists, output
  rows. Better cache locality.

  Multi-term intersection within partition

  - Intersect 2 posting lists at a time
  - For N terms, build balanced tree of merges
  - Parallel merges of disjoint pairs

  Posting list decode

  - Roaring containers independent → decode in parallel
  - Within a container, SIMD lanes (see below)

  Scan-level

  - Multiple queries on same index concurrently → block cache must be
  thread-safe (RW lock, fine-grained shards)

  Anti-parallelism

  - Tiny posting lists (<1024) → overhead > work. Single-thread.
  - Highly skewed selectivity (one term has 90% of work) → static partitioning
  bad, use work-stealing

  SIMD

  Posting list intersection

  Sorted uint32 arrays:

  SIMD galloping merge:
    Load 8 elements from list A (AVX2: 256-bit)
    Load 8 elements from list B
    pairwise compare → mask
    store matches via _mm256_maskcompress

  - AVX2: 8-wide on uint32. ~5-10x scalar speedup typical.
  - AVX-512: 16-wide. Even better, but availability varies.
  - ARM NEON: 4-wide. Decent.

  Roaring dense containers (bitsets):

  AND of two 8KB bitsets = 1024 _mm256_and_si256 ops
  → ~256 ns on modern CPU, fully SIMD

  - Most SIMD-friendly path. Pure bitwise.

  Roaring sparse containers (uint16 arrays):

  - Same galloping pattern as uint32 but 16-wide on AVX2 (16 elements per
  register).

  Posting list scan / cardinality

  - popcount on bitset chunks → _mm_popcnt_u64 per 8 bytes
  - AVX-512 has vpopcntq for parallel popcounts

  Dictionary block search

  - Front-coded block → strcmp can't SIMD across variable-length strings
  - Workaround: search hash table of first N chars per entry. SIMD compare 16
  chars at once.
  - Or: binary search top of block, linear scan within front-coded leaf (3-5
  entries) — branch predictor wins

  Decoding varint / delta

  - AVX-512 BMI for varint decode (PEXT/PDEP)
  - Stream VByte / MaskedVByte — SIMD varint variants
  - For roaring path → skip varint, use bitset directly. Faster.

  CRoaring already does most of this

  - AVX2/AVX-512 paths built in
  - Dynamic dispatch at runtime
  - Don't reinvent. Use library.

  Cache hierarchy

  L1 (32-64KB)

  - Hot path data structures fit here. Posting list chunks 8KB (roaring
  container) → 4-8 fit.
  - Block size aligned to cache line (64 bytes)
  - Avoid pointer chasing in hot loop (linear scan beats std::map due to L1
  hits)

  L2 (256KB-1MB)

  - Working set per partition fits here
  - Multiple posting list containers + dict block

  L3 (shared, MB-GB)

  - Recently accessed partitions
  - Cross-thread sharing here — keep block cache here

  RAM

  - All hot partitions
  - Block cache backing store
  - LRU eviction to disk when over budget

  SSD

  - Cold partitions
  - Pinned blocks via mmap (kernel page cache helps)
  - Sequential reads cheap, random reads expensive — block layout matters

  Memory hierarchy strategies

  Data layout

  - Struct-of-arrays for batch ops — separate term IDs, doc frequencies, posting
  list pointers in dict block. SIMD-friendly columns.
  - Hot/cold split — partition metadata (zone maps, term count) tiny +
  always-loaded vs dict/posting blocks (large + on-demand)
  - 64-byte alignment for SIMD loads + cache line alignment
  - Avoid false sharing — atomic counters padded to cache line

  Block size tuning

  ┌─────────────────┬──────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
  │      Block      │     Size     │                Reason                 │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Roaring         │ 8KB          │ matches typical L1, fits in cache     │
  │ container       │              │                                       │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Dict block      │ 4KB          │ page-aligned, OS-friendly, ~50 terms  │
  │                 │              │ front-coded                           │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Posting block   │ 4-64KB       │ trade between seek and decode cost    │
  │ chunk           │              │                                       │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Partition file  │ up to ~120K  │ matches DuckDB row group              │
  │                 │ rows         │                                       │
  └─────────────────┴──────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘

  Prefetching

  // Manual prefetch for next posting list block while decoding current
  __builtin_prefetch(next_block_ptr, 0, 1);

  - Software prefetch for predictable strides (sequential block reads)
  - Hardware prefetcher handles linear scans automatically
  - For pointer-chasing structures (B-tree, FST) → prefetch one ahead

  Memory bandwidth

  - Modern CPU: 30-50 GB/s. SIMD intersection saturates compute →
  bandwidth-bound on small lists
  - Roaring bitwise AND on bitsets → bandwidth-bound, ~200ns per 8KB pair
  - Decompression on read path → adds CPU work to bandwidth-bound op. Often wins
  because less bytes moved.

  NUMA

  - DuckDB doesn't currently NUMA-pin. Skip optimization unless multi-socket use
  case.
  - If needed: per-socket block cache, thread-affinity-aware partition
  assignment.

  Specific structure choices

  Dict lookup hot path

  Bad: std::map<string, idx_t>
  - Red-black tree, pointer chasing, cache misses per node
  - 8-12 cycles per node traversal due to L1 miss
  - Current implementation. Slow.

  Better: sorted vector<DictEntry> with binary search
  - Linear memory, contiguous
  - Binary search: log N comparisons, each in cache after first
  - 2-3x faster than std::map

  Best: SSTable block + sparse index
  - Sparse index in L2 (small, always hot)
