  - Block load → 4KB → 1-2 cache misses
  - Linear scan within block (front-coded, ~50 entries) → L1 hits

  Posting list hot path

  Bad: vector<uint32_t> scan for AND
  - Linear scan, no skip
  - 4 bytes per entry, 4MB per million entries
  - L2/L3 capable, slow on cold

  Better: roaring bitmap
  - Per-chunk optimal representation
  - Bitwise AND on bitsets → SIMD, ~100x faster than scalar
  - Galloping merge on sparse arrays
  - Built-in skip via chunk structure

  Best: roaring + skip pointers + SIMD-optimized intersection
  - CRoaring provides this out of the box

  Intersection algorithm

  For 2 posting lists A (size m), B (size n):

  - m, n similar → galloping merge SIMD, O(m+n)
  - m << n → binary-search each element of A in B, O(m log n)
  - m, n both huge dense → bitset AND, O(m/64) per word

  For K terms: order by selectivity ascending, intersect smallest with next,
  etc. Pruning early.

  Result materialization

  - Output row IDs into pre-allocated buffer
  - Avoid vector::push_back (potential realloc)
  - Use reserve(estimated_count) upfront

  Concrete optimizations to apply

  Now (low effort, big win)

  1. Replace std::map dict with sorted vector<DictEntry> + binary search — 2-3x
  dict lookup speedup
  2. Use roaring bitmaps for posting lists — 10x+ intersection on large lists,
  future-proof for NOT/positional
  3. Parallel partition scan — each thread owns full partition workflow, no
  shared state
  4. Reserve buffers upfront — estimated_count is known at plan time

  Soon

  5. CRoaring integration — pulls in AVX2 intersection paths
  6. Prefetch next posting block during decode of current
  7. 64-byte align hot structures — dict blocks, posting containers
  8. Block cache with RW lock sharding — 16 shards by block ID hash

  Later

  9. Compressed in-memory posting lists — fit more in L2/L3
  10. Profile-guided block size tuning — measure cache miss rates
  11. AVX-512 paths via runtime dispatch — gated on __builtin_cpu_supports
  12. Bloom filter pre-check before dict lookup — skip cold dict block load

  Anti-patterns to avoid

  - std::unordered_map in hot path — pointer chasing, fragmentation
  - std::string for tokens — 24+ bytes overhead, heap allocation
  - Branch-heavy inner loops — break SIMD opportunities. Use _mm256_blendv_epi8
  style masking.
  - Virtual functions in hot path — indirect calls, no inlining. Use static
  dispatch via templates.
  - Locks in scan loop — lock-free reads from block cache via shared_ptr or
  atomic refcount

  Measurement

  - Use perf stat for cycles, cache misses, instructions/cycle
  - Target: 2+ IPC on intersection loop (modern CPU = 4 wide, SIMD = 8 lanes ×
  throughput)
  - Cache miss rate < 5% on hot path → L1/L2 friendly
  - Branch mispredict < 2% → predictable control flow

  Tools to use

  - CRoaring — battle-tested, AVX2/AVX-512 ready
  - xxHash — fast non-crypto hash for bloom filters / sharding
  - DuckDB's existing SIMD primitives — simd_*.hpp in source, reuse rather than
  reinvent
  - xsimd or std::experimental::simd — portable SIMD abstraction layer

  Bottom line: roaring + partition-level parallelism + SSTable dict gets you 80%
  of modern-hardware win. Rest is profiling-guided.

✻ Worked for 1m 22s

❯ How is the dictionary of the index accessed? It contains the terms (or        
  termIDs) and a pointer to the posting lists, maybe with some metadata. During 
  query processing, the term in the search (or multiple) are looked up and      
  their posting lists are somehow merged (AND, OR). For trailing-wildcard       
  queries, we have to scan the dictionary sequentially. But otherwise, the      
  terms can be pretty far apart in theory. So I guess it is good to get the     
  pointers to the posting list in term-alphabetical order, even if later the    
  processing is ordered by estimated cardinality in the common case. Now let's  
  assume we have a million terms in the dictionary. How big would the           
  dictionary be? Maybe ~64 bytes on average for a term plus 64 bytes for the    
  pointer, so 128 MB. This would easily fit into memory, no? What other         
  metadata do we need to store? How can data-oriented programming help? How can 
  the dictionary get bigger? Maybe if we have timestamps as terms or something  
  like that?                                                                    
  How can I quickly access the term in a dictionary?                            

⏺ Access patterns

  Per query type

  ┌────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │           Query            │     Access pattern     │       Cost       │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ contains(msg, 'error')     │ Point lookup, 1 term   │ 1 access         │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ contains(msg, 'connection  │ Point lookup, 2-N      │ N accesses       │
  │ timeout')                  │ terms                  │                  │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ prefix(msg, 'err')         │ Range scan from "err"  │ M sequential     │
  │                            │ forward                │ accesses         │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ LIKE '%error%'             │ Tokenized → point      │ 1 access         │
  │                            │ lookup                 │                  │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ LIKE '%ror' (suffix)       │ Full dict scan or      │ O(dict size)     │
  │                            │ reverse dict           │                  │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ LIKE '%err%' (middle)      │ Full dict scan or      │ O(dict size)     │
  │                            │ n-gram index           │                  │
  └────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┴──────────────────┘

  Common case = point lookup. Make it fast.
  Range scan must be fast. Sorted order non-negotiable.
  Full dict scan = rare. Cold path, acceptable to be slow.

  Order matters

  You're right — dictionary in alphabetical order:
  - Range scan (prefix) is sequential read
  - Point lookup is binary search (log N)
  - Build order from inserts is random, but sort on flush

  Processing order at runtime is selectivity-ascending (smallest posting list
  first → prune fastest). Look up all terms first (alphabetical-friendly), then
  reorder by doc_frequency for intersection.

  Size estimate revisited

  Your numbers are off. Let me redo for 1M terms.

  Per-term bytes

  ┌────────────────────┬─────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Component      │  Bytes  │                   Note                   │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Term string        │ ~15 avg │ log tokens are short ("error",           │
  │                    │         │ "timeout"). 64 too high.                 │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Term length        │ 1-2     │ varint                                   │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ doc_frequency      │ 4       │ uint32                                   │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Posting list       │ 6-8     │ offset into posting file                 │
  │ offset             │         │                                          │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Posting list type  │ 1       │ inline / roaring / delta                 │
  │ tag                │         │                                          │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Total raw          │ ~26     │                                          │
  │                    │ bytes   │                                          │
  └────────────────────┴─────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Storage forms

  ┌───────────────────────────┬────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
  │           Form            │ Size for   │             Notes              │
  │                           │  1M terms  │                                │
  ├───────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Naive map<string, Entry>  │ ~120 MB    │ malloc overhead, pointer       │
  │                           │            │ chasing, fragmentation. Bad.   │
  ├───────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ vector<DictEntry> with    │ ~30 MB     │ flat, no overhead. Good for    │
  │ inline strings            │            │ cache.                         │
  ├───────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SSTable + front-coding    │ ~10-15 MB  │ shared prefixes compressed.    │
  │                           │            │ Best.                          │
  ├───────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ FST                       │ ~3-8 MB    │ maximal compression, complex   │
  └───────────────────────────┴────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

  128 MB for 1M terms is way too high. Closer to 10-30 MB. Fits in L3 cache on
  modern CPU (32-96 MB).

  What grows dictionary

  - Free-form IDs as tokens (UUIDs, trace_ids, request_ids) → each unique = new
  term. Worst offender. Million unique UUIDs → tokenizer splits → millions of
  terms.
  - High-cardinality user_id, session_id similar
  - Tokenized URLs with random params
  - Stack traces with line numbers / hashes
  - Timestamps if tokenized as strings (don't do this)
  - N-grams (if added later) — 2-gram from English ~~ 26² = 676; from log
  content with digits ~~ many thousands
  - Misspellings, typos in user-generated content

  For log workload realistic: 50K-500K unique terms per partition. 1M is high
  end.

  When dictionary won't fit

  - 100M unique terms (e.g., per-row trace IDs)
  - → Spill to disk via SSTable block cache
  - → Sparse index in RAM (1 entry per ~50 terms = 2M entries × 16 bytes = 32 MB
  for 100M terms)

  Metadata per term

  Essential

  struct DictEntry {
      uint32_t term_string_offset;  // into block string buffer
      uint16_t term_length;
      uint32_t doc_frequency;       // for selectivity check at plan time
      uint64_t posting_list_offset; // into posting list file
      uint32_t posting_list_size;   // bytes, for read sizing
      uint8_t  posting_list_encoding; // 0=inline, 1=roaring, 2=delta, ...
  };

  24 bytes per entry without string. Strings separate in block.

  Optional / future

      uint32_t collection_frequency; // total occurrences (for BM25)
      uint32_t max_doc_id;           // skip whole list if query bounded
      uint32_t min_doc_id;
      uint16_t position_list_offset; // for phrase queries
      uint16_t skip_table_offset;    // for fast intersection

  Per-partition metadata (separate, tiny)

  struct PartitionMeta {
      uint64_t row_start;
      uint32_t row_count;
      int64_t  ts_min, ts_max;
      uint32_t total_terms;
      uint32_t total_postings;
      uint64_t dict_file_offset;
      uint64_t posting_file_offset;
      BloomFilter coarse_bloom; // optional, ~1KB
  };

  Always in memory.

  Data-oriented design benefits

  Struct-of-arrays for dict

  Bad (AOS):
  DictEntry entries[1M];  // 24 bytes each, scattered access

  Good (SOA):
  uint32_t term_offsets[1M];
  uint16_t term_lengths[1M];
  uint32_t doc_freqs[1M];
  uint64_t posting_offsets[1M];

  Selectivity check at plan time → only scan doc_freqs[]. 4 bytes × 1M = 4 MB
  sequential. Fast. Other columns not touched.

  If AOS: same scan reads 24 bytes per entry = 24 MB. 6x slower.

  SIMD-friendly columns

  - doc_freqs[] as uint32[] → SIMD compare against threshold in one pass
  - term_lengths[] as uint16[] → SIMD filter for fixed-length terms

  Branch-free hot loop

  // AOS — branches per entry
  for (entry : entries) {
      if (entry.doc_freq > threshold) skip;
      if (entry.term_length != target_len) skip;
      ...
  }

  // SOA — vectorized
  for (i in range) {
      mask_freq = doc_freqs[i:i+8] > threshold;  // SIMD
      mask_len = term_lengths[i:i+8] == target_len;
      output[count] = i;
      count += popcount(mask_freq & mask_len);
  }

  Quick term access — methods compared

  Method 1: Sorted vector<DictEntry> + binary search

  auto it = std::lower_bound(entries.begin(), entries.end(), target,
                            [](const DictEntry &e, string_view t) {
                                return get_term(e) < t;
                            });

  - log₂(1M) = 20 comparisons
  - Each compare needs term string load (random access into string buffer)
  - L2/L3 hits after warmup
  - ~200-500 ns per lookup

  Method 2: Sparse index + block + linear scan within block

  // Sparse index (1 entry per 64 terms)
  auto block_idx = binary_search(sparse_index, target);  // log(N/64)
  auto block = load_block(block_idx);                     // 4KB cache line, ~50
  entries
  auto entry = linear_scan(block, target);                // <50 compares, all 
  L1

  - 14 comparisons in sparse index + 50 in block = 64 ops
  - Block load is contiguous → 1 cache miss, rest L1
  - ~100-200 ns per lookup

  Method 3: Hash table

  auto it = hash_map.find(target);

  - O(1) expected
  - Bad: no prefix scan
  - Bad: cache misses on collision chains
  - ~50-100 ns per lookup, but loses prefix capability

  Method 4: Hybrid — hash for exact + sorted for prefix

  unordered_map<string, idx_t> exact_lookup;     // for point queries
  vector<DictEntry>            sorted_entries;   // for prefix scan

  - Best of both
  - Double memory cost (hash table + sorted vector)
  - Exact: ~50 ns, prefix: same as sorted

  Method 5: FST

  auto offset = fst.lookup(target);

  - O(|term|) — bytes in term
  - Compressed, mmap-friendly
  - Supports prefix scan
  - ~50-200 ns, depending on term length and FST depth

  Method 6: Trie / radix tree

  - O(|term|) lookup
  - Prefix scan natural
  - Memory-heavy if not compressed
  - Pointer chasing → cache-unfriendly

  Recommendation

  Sparse index + sorted block + linear scan (Method 2):
  - Fastest of the simple options
  - Supports prefix scan natively
  - Spillable (blocks are 4KB on disk)
  - Cache-friendly (block load = 1 miss)
  - Linear scan within block is SIMD-able
  - ~50 terms per block × 20K blocks = 1M terms
  - Sparse index 20K entries × 16 bytes = 320 KB, fits L2

  Future: layer hash table on top for exact lookups if profile shows lookup is
  bottleneck.

  Concrete dict layout

  In-memory (always loaded)

  struct InMemoryDict {
      // Sparse index — first term per block + block offset
      struct SparseEntry {
          uint64_t first_term_hash;  // for fast non-equal compare
          uint32_t first_term_offset; // into string region
          uint16_t first_term_length;
          uint32_t block_offset;     // in dict file
      };
      vector<SparseEntry> sparse;

      // String pool for sparse entries' first terms
      vector<char> string_pool;

      // Stats
      uint32_t total_terms;
  };

  On-disk dict block (4KB, loaded on demand)

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Block header                            │
  │   num_entries (uint16)                  │
  │   strings_offset (uint16)               │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SOA arrays:                             │
  │   doc_frequency[num_entries]            │
  │   posting_offset[num_entries]           │
  │   posting_size[num_entries]             │
  │   posting_encoding[num_entries]         │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Front-coded string region:              │
  │   shared_prefix_len, suffix_len, suffix │
  │   ...                                   │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Optional: trailing block CRC32C         │
  └────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Block size tuning

  - 4KB matches OS page → mmap-friendly
  - 50 entries per block (avg 80 bytes per entry including term string)
  - 1M terms = 20K blocks = 80 MB on disk
  - Sparse index 20K × 24 bytes = 480 KB in memory
  - Block cache 256-1024 entries = 1-4 MB. LRU.

  Term-ID indirection

  If posting lists reference terms many times, use term IDs not strings:

  dict:  "error" → term_id 0
         "info"  → term_id 1

  posting list payload references term_id when storing context (positions, etc)

  Saves bytes when terms appear in many places (positions, snippets).

  For our v1: posting lists only store row IDs, not term refs. Skip term IDs.

  Bloom filter pre-check

  Tiny per-partition bloom (~1KB) gives "term definitely not present" answer in
  50 ns without dict lookup.

  For queries hitting many partitions, most have most terms absent. Bloom skips
  dict load.

  if (!partition.bloom.maybe_contains(term)) {
      continue;  // skip dict lookup entirely
  }
  // proceed to dict lookup

  Cheap win, worth doing.

  What you might be missing

  1. String pool deduplication — same term in many partitions. Each partition
  stores its own. For 1M tokens across 1000 partitions, redundant.
  Cross-partition dedup is complex; defer.
  2. Term frequency stats vs collection frequency — doc_frequency = number of
  docs containing term. collection_frequency = total occurrences. BM25 needs
  both. Store only df for v1.
  3. Plan-time vs execution-time stats — doc_frequency is global, useful at plan
  time for selectivity. At execution time you need per-partition doc_frequency
  to decide skip order. Store both? Yes — partition-level is cheap.
  4. Compression of doc_frequency — many terms have df=1 (long tail). Use
  varint. Saves 60-80%.
  5. Encoding tag per term — small posting list inline in dict entry vs separate
  file. Saves indirection for short lists.
  6. Type tags for term origin — if doing multi-column, prefix terms
  "message:error", "service:api". Bytes count.
  7. Soft term limits — cap dict size per partition; spill overflow terms to
  "rare terms" auxiliary structure. Bounds memory.
  8. TermID stability — if you assign term IDs sequentially during build,
  they're partition-local. Don't expose them externally.
  9. Pre-tokenized search term — query "error timeout" tokenizes to ["error",
  "timeout"]. Lookups should reuse tokenizer.

  Final dict access numbers

  ┌────────────────────────┬───────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │       Operation        │     Time      │             Notes             │
  ├────────────────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Point lookup (hot)     │ 100-200 ns    │ sparse index + block linear   │
  │                        │               │ scan                          │
  ├────────────────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Point lookup (cold,    │ 100-500 μs    │ block read from SSD           │
  │ disk)                  │               │                               │
  ├────────────────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Prefix range start     │ 100-200 ns    │ binary search + block load    │
  ├────────────────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Prefix range step      │ 5-20 ns       │ within block, linear          │
  ├────────────────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Bloom check            │ 30-50 ns      │ preempts cold loads           │
  ├────────────────────────┼───────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Full scan (suffix)     │ 100-500 ms /  │ rare, acceptable              │
  │                        │ 1M            │                               │
  └────────────────────────┴───────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  Bottom line: 1M-term dict = ~30 MB AOS or ~10 MB SSTable + sparse index.
  Easily fits RAM. Point lookup ~100 ns hot. Don't worry about dict memory at
  this scale. Worry about access pattern fitting cache.

✻ Baked for 1m 34s

❯ Coming back to "Don't optimize for analytics over indexed column (use raw     
  scan + zone maps)".                                                           
  DuckDB doesn't have bloom filters in its storage. I wonder if our index could 
  help with queries that filter on a low-cardinality attribute like the         
  "service" where logs are coming from or "environment" (dev/staging/prod).     
                                                                                
  For SIMD, we should use auto-vectorization.                                   
                                                                                
  Speaking of cold storage: Eventually we should build a PRAGMA function to     
  remove index entries that are older than N days. And have a maximum_retention 
  option. But this is for later.                                                

⏺ Indexing low-cardinality attributes

  You're right — I conflated two things:

  - Analytics over indexed column (SELECT service, count(*) GROUP BY service) —
  no filter, full scan needed. Index can't help. Use zone maps + columnar scan.
  - Filtering on low-cardinality (WHERE service = 'api') — different problem.
  Index CAN help.

  Why DuckDB scan is slow here

  - Zone maps useless: every row group contains every service value
  - No bloom filters in DuckDB storage
  - → Full scan, predicate eval per row

  How index helps

  Multi-column logsearch:

  CREATE INDEX idx ON logs USING logsearch(message, service, environment, level)

  WITH (timestamp_column = 'ts');

  Internal dict entries with column prefix:
  "message:error"      → posting list A
  "message:timeout"    → posting list B
  "service:api"        → posting list C (LARGE — 20% of rows)
  "service:web"        → posting list D
  "environment:prod"   → posting list E (LARGE — 90% of rows)
  "environment:dev"    → posting list F
  "level:error"        → posting list G

  Compression matters here

  Low-cardinality posting list = dense. Roaring uses bitset container → 8KB per
  64K rows regardless of density.

  For 100M rows, service:api (20%) = 20M row IDs:
  - Raw uint32[]: 80 MB
  - Roaring bitset chunks: ~12 MB
  - 7x smaller. Fits cache hierarchy better.

  Selectivity revisited

  Single low-cardinality predicate alone:
  - service = 'api' → 20% selectivity → above 20% threshold → skip index → scan

  But compound:
  - service = 'api' AND contains(msg, 'timeout') → intersect
  - timeout posting list: 0.1% (100K rows)
  - service:api posting list: 20% (20M rows)
  - Intersection: ~20K rows
  - Cost: bitwise AND on dense chunks where service:api is dense →
  SIMD-friendly, fast
  - Result: 20K row IDs, fetch only those

  This is the killer use case for combining text + structured filters. DuckDB
  can't do this with zone maps or bloom filters. Our index can.

  Optimizer changes needed

  Currently match: contains(col, 'x'), prefix(col, 'x'), col = 'x' (text only).

  Add: col = 'x' where col is any indexed column. Look up "col_name:value" in
  dict.

  For multi-column compound: intersect posting lists across columns. Already
  works at posting list level — just need optimizer to emit the intersection.

  Threshold revisit

  20% threshold too strict for compound queries. Need new rule:

  - If predicate alone has high selectivity → skip
  - If predicate is part of compound AND with selective predicate → use
  - Decision: lookup all term doc_frequencies, sort ascending, intersect until
  selectivity good enough, post-filter rest

  This is classic IR query optimization (WAND, MaxScore algorithms in Lucene).

  Storage cost

  Low-cardinality terms add few entries to dict but very large posting lists.
  Roaring keeps them manageable.

  For 5 services, 3 environments, 4 levels, 100M rows:
  - 12 extra dict entries (negligible)
  - Posting lists: ~12 × 12 MB = 144 MB
  - Total index size grows by ~25% (assuming message posting lists ~600 MB)

  Worth it for query speedup.

  Auto-vectorization

  Right call. Hand-written intrinsics are maintenance burden + portability
  problem. Let compiler do it.

  What compilers do well

  - Simple loops with linear access
  - Fixed iteration count or count parameter
  - No branches in body (or branches predictable)
  - POD types
  - restrict pointers
  - Aligned data

  Make it happen

  Compiler flags:
  -O3 -march=native            # native SIMD width
  or
  -O3 -mavx2                   # specific ISA

  For extension distribution, pick baseline (e.g., AVX2 universal on x86-64-v3)
  + runtime dispatch for AVX-512 critical paths.

  Code patterns:

  // Good — auto-vectorizable
  void intersect(const uint32_t * __restrict a, size_t na,
                 const uint32_t * __restrict b, size_t nb,
                 uint32_t * __restrict out, size_t &n_out) {
      size_t i = 0, j = 0, k = 0;
      while (i < na && j < nb) {
          // Branch but predictable; compiler may use cmov
          if (a[i] == b[j]) { out[k++] = a[i]; i++; j++; }
          else if (a[i] < b[j]) i++;
          else j++;
      }
      n_out = k;
  }

  Actually merge intersection has data-dependent control flow — hard to
  auto-vectorize. CRoaring uses hand-written SIMD here.

  Better targets for auto-vectorization:

  // Bitset AND — trivially vectorizable
  void bitset_and(const uint64_t * __restrict a,
                  const uint64_t * __restrict b,
                  uint64_t * __restrict out, size_t n_words) {
      for (size_t i = 0; i < n_words; i++) {
          out[i] = a[i] & b[i];
      }
      // Compiler emits AVX2: vpand on 4× uint64 per instruction
  }

  // Selectivity column scan
  size_t count_above(const uint32_t * __restrict freqs, size_t n, uint32_t 
  threshold) {
      size_t count = 0;
      for (size_t i = 0; i < n; i++) {
          count += (freqs[i] > threshold);  // branchless, vectorizes
      }
      return count;
  }

  // Popcount over bitset
  size_t popcount_bitset(const uint64_t * __restrict bs, size_t n) {
      size_t c = 0;
      for (size_t i = 0; i < n; i++) c += __builtin_popcountll(bs[i]);
      return c;
  }

  Annotations to use

  - __restrict__ (or __restrict) on pointers
  - [[likely]] / [[unlikely]] on branches
  - __builtin_assume(n % 4 == 0) for hints
  - #pragma GCC ivdep (GCC) — assume no loop-carried dependencies
  - #pragma clang loop vectorize(enable) (Clang)
  - alignas(64) on hot data structures

  Verification

  Compile with -fopt-info-vec (GCC) or -Rpass=loop-vectorize (Clang). Reports
  which loops vectorized + why others didn't.

  Where auto-vec falls short

  - Galloping intersection of sparse arrays — control flow too data-dependent
  - Variable-length string compare — strcmp can't vectorize across boundaries
  - Roaring container dispatch — virtual-like switch hurts

  → Use CRoaring (has hand-tuned SIMD where compiler can't) + auto-vec for
  everything else.

  Plan

  1. Default: write straightforward loops, SoA layout, restrict pointers,
  alignment
  2. Compile with -O3 -march=x86-64-v3 baseline + check -fopt-info-vec output
  3. Hot paths that don't vectorize: use CRoaring (already hand-tuned) or accept
  scalar
  4. Avoid intrinsics in our code — keeps portable to ARM/RISC-V

  Retention PRAGMA — note for later

  Good idea. Add to backlog. Sketch:

  PRAGMA logsearch_prune_index('idx_name', '7 days');

  CREATE INDEX idx ON logs USING logsearch(message)
      WITH (timestamp_column = 'ts', max_retention = '7 days');

  Implementation:
  - Per-partition ts_max already stored
  - Iterate partitions, drop those with ts_max < now() - retention
  - Free dictionary + posting list memory
  - Atomic with respect to ongoing queries (RW lock on partitions list)
  - Background option: cron-like hook fires periodically

  Doesn't touch source table — just frees index metadata. Reasonable.

  Future:
  - Auto-prune triggered on INSERT if max_retention set
  - Coordinate with DELETE WHERE ts < cutoff on source table

  Document as v2 feature. v1: manual drop + rebuild.

  Updated priorities given this conversation

  1. Multi-column indexing with key-prefix ("service:api") — high value,
  moderate effort
  2. Roaring bitmaps via CRoaring — high value, low effort (vendor library)
  3. Compound predicate optimizer rule (intersect across columns) — high value,
  medium effort
  4. Auto-vectorizable bitset ops + selectivity column scan — easy wins
  5. Retention PRAGMA — defer to v2
  6. FST dictionary — defer to v3 (SSTable enough)

  What you may be missing on low-cardinality

  - Cardinality detection at build time — count distinct values per column. If <
  threshold (say 10K), automatically include in index. If > threshold, skip
  (too expensive, use ART instead).
  - Boolean columns — special case of low-cardinality. Posting list per value is
  fine.
  - NULL handling — separate "is_null" posting list? Or filter at fetch? Define
  semantics.
  - Per-column tokenization — text column gets tokenized, exact-match columns
  don't. Need config.
  - Update story — service rarely changes per row, but if it does, posting list
  update is needed. For append-only logs, irrelevant.
  - Histogram alternative — for high-cardinality with skew (one user_id has 90%
  of rows), even posting list is huge. Consider hybrid: top-K values get posting
  lists, rest fall through.

  Bottom line: indexing low-cardinality columns is the right move for log
  search. Killer feature DuckDB lacks. Worth implementing in v2 right after
  roaring.

✻ Brewed for 1m 27s

❯ What exactly is a partition in our case?                                      

⏺ Partition = inverted index slice for one DuckDB row group

  Definition

  One IndexPartition ↔ one row group in DuckDB's RowGroupCollection.

  What it contains

  class IndexPartition {
      idx_t row_start;            // global row ID of first row in row group
      idx_t row_count;             // number of rows (typically ~122K)

      timestamp_t ts_min, ts_max;  // from zone maps of timestamp column
      bool has_timestamp_bounds;

      map<string, idx_t> dictionary_;       // term → posting list index
      vector<PostingList> posting_lists_;   // sorted local row offsets 
  (uint32_t)
  };

  Local vs global row IDs

  - Posting list stores local offsets (0 to ~122K, fits in uint32_t)
  - Global row ID = row_start + local_offset
  - Saves memory: uint32_t instead of int64_t
  - Bounded size enables fixed-width packing

  Boundaries from GetPartitionStats()

  During build:
  auto stats = storage.GetPartitionStats(context);  // public API
  for (auto &ps : stats) {
      row_start = ps.row_start.GetIndex();
      row_count = ps.count;
      // ps.partition_row_group->GetColumnStatistics(ts_storage_idx)
      //   → ts_min, ts_max
  }

  Why partition = row group

  - DuckDB row group = ~122K rows, contiguous row IDs, immutable column data,
  has zone maps
  - Natural alignment for time-based pruning (logs ordered by ts → each row
  group covers narrow ts range)
  - Independent: build in parallel, drop independently, serialize independently
  - Bounded size: dict + posting lists fit in cache predictably
  - Matches DuckDB's own pruning unit (zone map check is per row group)

  Lifetime

  - Created during build_global_init (one per existing row group at index time)
  - Filled during build_sink (route each row to its partition by row_id binary
  search)
  - New partition created in Append() when new rows fall outside existing
  partition ranges
  - Dropped on retention prune (ts_max < cutoff)
  - Reset on full index rebuild

  Not what partition is

  - Not a DuckDB physical block
  - Not a "fragment" in Husky sense (which is also row-group-like, name overlap)
  - Not related to PARTITION BY SQL clause
  - Not a logical date partition the user creates

  Operations on partition

  - Query: lookup terms in dict, return posting lists
  - Skip: if [ts_min, ts_max] ∩ query range = ∅
  - Merge: combine two partitions with same row_start (parallel build → combine
  phase)
  - Add row: tokenize, append local offset to posting lists
  - Drop: free dict + posting lists, remove from partition list

  Constraints

  - All row IDs in partition: [row_start, row_start + row_count)
  - Posting list offsets: [0, row_count)
  - Posting list sorted by offset (monotonic invariant)
  - Dictionary terms unique within partition

  Why this granularity wins

  - 1M rows / 122K per partition = ~8 partitions → small fan-out
  - 100M rows = ~800 partitions → still manageable
  - 10B rows = ~80K partitions → need partition metadata index, but doable
  - Time pruning skips partitions wholesale (1 comparison per partition vs
  row-by-row)
  - Per-partition dict size bounded → cache-friendly

  Tied to DuckDB row group lifecycle

  - Row group split (rare) → would need to split partition. Currently not
  supported. Note as limitation.
  - Row group merge (compaction) → invalidates index. Currently no hook. Note.
  - Row group delete (retention) → corresponding partition should drop. Not
  auto. Manual via PRAGMA later.

  Bottom line: partition = unit of inverted index aligned with DuckDB row group.
  Natural, bounded, independent, time-prunable.

✻ Cogitated for 49s

❯ When a query is processed, how does it find relevant partitions? Isn't there  
  overhead through having to merge many posting lists compared to having a      
  larger postings list for multiple row groups?                                 

⏺ Finding relevant partitions

  Index-level structure

  LogsearchIndex has vector<unique_ptr<IndexPartition>> sorted by row_start.

  class LogsearchIndex : public BoundIndex {
      vector<unique_ptr<IndexPartition>> partitions;  // sorted by row_start
  };

  Per-query flow

  vector<row_t> LogsearchIndex::SearchTerms(terms, ts_min, ts_max, has_ts) {
      vector<row_t> result;
      for (auto &p : partitions) {
          // 1. Timestamp prune
          if (has_ts && !p->OverlapsTimestampRange(ts_min, ts_max)) continue;

          // 2. (Future) Bloom check
          if (!p->bloom.MayContain(terms[0])) continue;

          // 3. Lookup each term in dict
          vector<PostingList*> lists;
          for (auto &t : terms) {
              auto *pl = p->Search(t);
              if (!pl) { lists.clear(); break; }  // term absent → no match in 
  partition
              lists.push_back(pl);
          }
          if (lists.empty()) continue;

          // 4. Intersect within partition
          auto partition_result = IntersectAll(lists);

          // 5. Convert local offsets to global row IDs, append
          for (auto offset : partition_result) {
              result.push_back(p->row_start + offset);
          }
      }
      return result;  // sorted globally because partitions sorted, offsets 
  sorted within
  }

  Steps per partition

  1. TS check — 2 timestamp compares, ~ns. Branch predicts.
  2. Bloom check (future) — 50 ns per term.
  3. Dict lookup — 100-200 ns per term (sparse index + block scan).
  4. Intersection — N-way merge of small lists, cache-friendly.
  5. Conversion — row_start + offset per result row.

  Iteration order

  Currently linear scan of partitions. For 800 partitions:
  - 800 × (2 compare + early exit on ts) = 1600 compares ≈ 5 μs total to skip
  - Negligible

  For 80K partitions:
  - 80K × 2 compare = 160K ops ≈ 500 μs
  - Becomes noticeable
  - Solution: binary search by ts_max on sorted partitions (logs ordered by ts →
  partitions ordered too) → log(80K) = 17 ops

  Skip optimization for log workload

  // Find first partition with ts_max >= query.ts_min via binary search
  auto start = std::lower_bound(partitions.begin(), partitions.end(),
  query.ts_min,
      [](auto &p, ts_t ts) { return p->ts_max < ts; });

  // Find first partition with ts_min > query.ts_max
  auto end = std::upper_bound(start, partitions.end(), query.ts_max,
      [](ts_t ts, auto &p) { return ts < p->ts_min; });

  for (auto it = start; it != end; ++it) { ... }

  O(log N) discovery, O(matching) iteration.

  Partition-level vs global posting list — overhead analysis

  Hypothesis: "fewer, larger posting lists = less merge overhead"

  Tempting. Wrong for our workload. Reasons below.

  What you'd save with single global posting list per term

  - 1 dict lookup instead of N (where N = partitions touched)
  - 1 contiguous posting list read instead of N
  - Single decompression

  What you'd lose

  1. Timestamp pruning

  - Partition: skip whole partition via 2 compares. Touch 0 bytes.
  - Global: must scan/decode posting list, filter by row ID → ts range
  conversion. Touch entire list.
  - For typical query (ts > now() - 1 hour, retention 30 days): partition prunes
  ~99% of work. Global has zero pruning here.
  - This is the biggest win for partitions.

  2. Selectivity per partition

  - "rare" globally but common in recent partition? Partition lets you target.
  - Hot partitions cached, cold partitions on disk — partition granularity
  matches cache granularity.

  3. Compression effectiveness

  - Roaring bitmap chunks by upper 16 bits of row ID. Inside one partition, row
  IDs span 122K (about 2 chunks). Dense within chunks.
  - Global posting list spans 0 to billions. Same row IDs distributed across
  many chunks. Each chunk less dense.
  - Roaring compresses better when row IDs clustered → partition-aligned wins.

  4. Build parallelism

  - Partition build: each thread builds independent partition, no
  synchronization
  - Global build: contention on shared dict + posting lists, locking overhead

  5. Append cost

  - Partition: append to "tail" partition only, others untouched
  - Global: append modifies giant posting list, potentially compressed →
  decompress, append, recompress

  6. Retention drop

  - Partition: free old partitions wholesale (cheap)
  - Global: delete entries from middle of list → rewrite (expensive)

  7. Memory pressure

  - Partition: evict cold partitions individually
  - Global: all-or-nothing in memory

  8. Cache locality during intersection

  - Partition: posting list per partition fits in L1/L2 (small)
  - Global: posting list 100x larger, exceeds cache, slow scan

  Hard numbers

  Workload: 1 billion rows, 800 partitions (~1.2M each), query for term with
  global df=10K (rare).

  Per-partition df distribution typical: Pareto-like. Few partitions have many
  matches; most have few/zero.

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Approach     │                      Operations                       │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                 │ 800 × ts-check = 1600 ops. Skip 750. Lookup term in   │
  │ Partition-level │ 50 partitions × 200ns = 10μs. Intersect small lists   │
  │                 │ (avg ~200 entries each) = 100μs. Total ~110 μs.       │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                 │ 1 dict lookup = 200ns. Load 10K-entry posting list =  │
  │ Global posting  │ 40KB scan + decompress. Filter by ts range (which     │
  │ list            │ requires checking each row_id) = 10K compares. Total  │
  │                 │ ~200 μs (no ts pruning savings).                      │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Partition wins. And the gap widens with more partitions (better selectivity
  granularity, better cache, better ts pruning).

  When global posting list would win

  - Tiny tables (< 1 row group): only 1 partition anyway, equivalent
  - Queries with no ts filter AND term in every partition: extra dict lookup per
  partition is pure overhead. Real but small (10K partitions × 200 ns = 2 ms —
  usually still cheaper than touching giant global list).
  - Pathological term distribution (term in 100% of partitions, df=row_count) →
  these terms are above selectivity threshold anyway → index not used.

  Multi-term intersection: per-partition or global?

  Per-partition wins because:

  - Smaller lists, intersection completes faster (cache + early termination)
  - Independent → parallelize across partitions
  - A term absent in partition → skip partition entirely (no work)
  - Globally: must read both giant lists, intersect, then filter by ts

  Cost we DO pay

  - Dict lookup per partition per term: 200 ns × P × T (P = partitions touched,
  T = terms)
  - For 800 partitions × 3 terms = 480 μs of dict overhead in worst case (no ts
  pruning)
  - Mitigation: bloom filter pre-check (50 ns per term skips dict load)
  - Mitigation: ts pruning typically eliminates 90%+ of partitions before dict
  lookup

  Where partitioning hurts measurably

  Query: WHERE contains(msg, 'apple') AND NOT contains(msg, 'banana')
  - Need full posting list of "banana" globally to complement
  - Partition: must union all "banana" partition lists first → same cost as
  global
  - Marginal case. NOT queries are rare.

  Verdict

  Partition granularity = right choice for log workload.

  Benefits dominate overhead:
  1. Timestamp pruning (huge for time-series)
  2. Cache locality (smaller per-partition working set)
  3. Build/append/drop parallelism
  4. Independent retention
  5. Better roaring compression

  Overhead (N × dict lookups) bounded by:
  - ts pruning (typical 90%+ skip)
  - Bloom filter (cheap skip)
  - Sorted partition order (binary search for time-bounded queries)

  When to revisit

  - If partition count > 100K → consider hierarchical partitioning (group of
  partitions per time bucket)
  - If queries have no ts predicate AND terms span all partitions → overhead
  becomes ~ms range. Consider global secondary index for very common terms.
  - If individual partition is too small (< 10K rows) → merge adjacent

  Tuning

  - Partition size = DuckDB row group size (~122K) is a good default
  - Could be tuned: bigger partitions = more cache pressure but less metadata
  overhead
  - DuckDB doesn't let you control row group size easily, so we inherit it

  What you might be missing

  - Partition-level fast path for "term absent everywhere" — global bloom across
  all partitions. If miss, skip all partition lookups. Tiny memory cost.
  - Partition skip when posting list empty — implicit if dict lookup misses
  - Cross-partition concatenation order — partition iteration order = increasing
  row_start = increasing row ID. Result naturally sorted. No final sort needed.
  - Result size estimation per partition — for early termination on LIMIT
  queries.

  Bottom line: many smaller posting lists has overhead, but partition-aligned
  posting lists give you ts-pruning, cache locality, and compression that more
  than compensate. Single-global approach loses time-range pruning entirely —
  fatal for log workload.
✻ Cooked for 1m 13s

❯  How does the incremental index build go together with DuckDB's INSERT? In    
  which row group does DuckDB write data? Is the index build only when a        
  transaction is committed or continuously? If before a commit, should we       
  already update the partition for the last row group?                          

⏺ DuckDB INSERT write path

  INSERT INTO logs ...
      ↓
  PhysicalInsert
      ↓
  LocalStorage::Append          ← per-transaction, invisible to others
      ↓ (rows written into LocalStorage's RowGroupCollection)
      ↓
  COMMIT
      ↓
  LocalStorage::Commit          ← merges local row groups into main DataTable
      ↓
  DataTable::RowGroupCollection ← visible to all transactions

  Where exactly rows land:

  - During INSERT: rows go to last row group of LocalStorage's row group 
  collection (per-txn). New row group appended when current fills (~122K rows).
  - On COMMIT: those local row groups appended to main DataTable.row_groups. Row
  IDs preserved from local assignment.
  - Row ID assignment: sequential continuation of main storage's row IDs at
  start of local append.

  Row groups in main row_groups collection are append-only. Last row group
  typically partial (< 122K rows). Fills over time as new commits append.

  When index sees writes

  Two paths in DuckDB:

  Path A: Write-through (our current behavior)

  - BoundIndex::Append called during INSERT, before commit
  - Each chunk written to LocalStorage also delivered to global index
  - Our SupportsDeltaIndexes() = default false → we get the write-through path
  - Visible to writer txn; visible to other txns via post-filter even before
  commit (problematic for strict isolation)

  Path B: Delta index (cleaner)

  - Override SupportsDeltaIndexes() = true + implement CreateDeltaIndex()
  - DuckDB creates per-txn delta index
  - INSERT writes go into delta index only
  - On COMMIT: delta merged into global via MergeIndexes
  - On ROLLBACK: delta discarded — index untouched
  - MVCC-correct: other txns don't see uncommitted data

  ART uses Path B for proper MVCC. We don't, currently.

  Should we update partition for last row group before commit?

  Yes — and we already do via Append. But the current handling is broken.

  Current broken flow

  void LogsearchIndex::AddRow(row_t row_id, const string_t &value) {
      auto *partition = FindPartition(row_id);
      if (!partition) {
          // Row outside known partitions — create singleton
          auto new_partition = make_uniq<IndexPartition>(row_id, 1);
          partition = new_partition.get();
          partitions.push_back(std::move(new_partition));  // ← problem
      }
      ...
  }

  Problems:

  1. New row group not yet finalized → not in our boundary list → create
  singleton partition with count=1
  2. Next row arrives → outside this singleton (row_id+1 > row_start+1) → create
  another singleton
  3. End up with thousands of micro-partitions for one new row group
  4. Doesn't capture proper row group bounds (zone maps not finalized either)
  5. After commit + checkpoint, real row group exists with proper bounds, but we
  have garbage

  Fix options

  Option 1: Map row_id → row group at insert time

  auto *partition = FindPartition(row_id);
  if (!partition) {
      // Query DuckDB for which row group this row belongs to
      auto rg = duck_table.GetStorage().GetRowGroupForId(row_id);
      if (rg) {
          // Create partition matching real row group bounds
          partition = CreatePartition(rg->row_start, rg->count);
      } else {
          // Brand new row group — create with conservative bounds
          // Extend on subsequent inserts that fall in same range
      }
  }

  Cleaner. But requires re-querying boundaries on partition miss.

  Option 2: Extend last partition (append-only assumption)

  auto &last = partitions.back();
  if (row_id >= last->row_start && row_id < last->row_start + ROW_GROUP_SIZE) {
      last->row_count = max(last->row_count, row_id - last->row_start + 1);
      AddTermsToLast(row_id, value);
  } else {
      // New row group — create new partition
      CreatePartition(row_id, /* known count? */);
  }

  Simple. Assumes monotonic appends. Works for logs.

  Option 3: Delta index — defer to commit

  bool SupportsDeltaIndexes() const override { return true; }
  unique_ptr<BoundIndex> CreateDeltaIndex(DeltaIndexType type) const override {
      return make_uniq<LogsearchIndex>(...);  // empty per-txn index
  }

  - Per-txn LogsearchIndex accumulates inserts
  - MergeIndexes called on commit, joins delta partitions into main
  - Rollback drops delta automatically
  - Cleaner semantics

  Recommendation

  Short term: Option 2. Extend last partition for monotonic appends. Matches log
  workload. Cheap fix.

  Medium term: Option 3. Delta index for MVCC correctness + clean rollback.
  Aligns with how ART works.

  Long term: Hybrid. Delta index per txn, commits trigger merge. Merge
  intelligently handles new row group creation.

  Zone maps and ts_min/ts_max timing

  Zone maps finalized when:
  - Row group fully written (filled to capacity)
  - On checkpoint
  - On explicit query of statistics

  For partial row groups (last one mid-fill), zone maps may be incomplete or
  expanded as rows added.

  For our index:
  - At build_global_init (CREATE INDEX): read zone maps via GetPartitionStats()
  → snapshot at index creation
  - At Append (subsequent INSERTs): zone maps not refreshed in our partition's
  ts_min/ts_max
  - Bug: partition's ts_max stale after new rows added beyond original max

  Fix: track ts_max of indexed values per partition, update on every Append.
  Tokenizer doesn't see ts column — need to pass ts as separate expression in
  index definition, or query zone maps on flush.

  Concrete recommendation

  For v1 maturity:

  1. Implement Option 2 (extend last partition). Simple fix for monotonic
  appends. Eliminates singleton partition bug.
  2. Track per-partition ts bounds on update. Either:
    - Index definition includes ts as second expression (passed to build_sink)
    - Or query zone maps via GetPartitionStats() on commit boundary
    - First option is cleaner
  3. Defer MVCC strict isolation to v2. For log workload, dirty reads of
  uncommitted log rows acceptable. Post-filter via Fetch() MVCC respects commit
  visibility.
  4. Document: index reflects writes immediately (not commit-bound). Note
  rollback caveat (entries remain but invisible via Fetch).

  Reality check on commit timing

  For logging workload typical:
  - Auto-commit per INSERT batch
  - Batches large (1000-10000 rows)
  - Rollback rare
  - Write-through is fine

  For transactional workload (multi-statement txn that may rollback):
  - Need delta index path
  - Important for correctness

  If we target "log search" specifically → write-through OK with extension
  partition fix.

  If we target "any DuckDB workload" → delta index needed.

  Summary

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          Q           │                        A                         │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Where does INSERT    │ LocalStorage row group collection; last row      │
  │ write?               │ group filled, new appended when full. On commit, │
  │                      │  merged into main DataTable.                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Index updated during │ Currently during txn (write-through via Append). │
  │  txn or commit?      │  Should be delta-on-commit for MVCC.             │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Update partition for │ Yes, but current code creates singleton          │
  │  last row group      │ partitions for unknown row IDs. Fix by extending │
  │ before commit?       │  last partition (Option 2) or going delta        │
  │                      │ (Option 3).                                      │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Zone maps fresh?     │ No — partition's ts_min/ts_max set at build, not │
  │                      │  refreshed on Append. Need to track or re-query. │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

⏺ README written.

  Export full conversation

  Two options:

  Option 1 — /export command (built-in)

  /export <filename>

  Already used earlier (/export convo.md →
  /Users/florian/MotherDuck/duckdb/convo.txt, /export convo2.txt → same dir).
  Dumps full transcript as plain text.

  Option 2 — Transcript JSON files (raw)

  Claude Code stores per-session JSONL transcripts. Location varies by version:
  ~/.claude/projects/<project-hash>/transcripts/
  or
  ~/.claude/transcripts/

  Find with:
  find ~/.claude -name "*.jsonl" -newer convo.txt 2>/dev/null

  JSONL has full message structure (system reminders, tool calls, results).
  Richer than /export but harder to read.

  Recommendation: /export complete-convo.md now. Plain text, readable, captures
  everything in this session.
